A Friend Asked: Essays on Self-as-an-End · Volume I

A Friend Asked, Volume I: From Instrumentalization to Self-as-an-End
有朋友问 · 卷一：从工具化到自我目的

Han Qin (秦汉) · 2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23959070 · Full PDF on Zenodo · CC BY 4.0
About this volume

A Friend Asked: Essays on Self-as-an-End, Volume I: From Instrumentalization to Self-as-an-End is a collection of applied-philosophy essays that begins with ordinary questions. This volume contains the general preface and sixty-five essays, arranged in the order in which they were originally written and organized into four parts: “Learning to Judge,” “The Expansion of Instrumentalization,” “The Person as an End,” and “The Framework Enters the World.”

Beginning with questions about risk, technology, finance, platforms, and popular culture, the essays gradually develop the central concern of Self-as-an-End (SAE): how people can resist treating themselves and others merely as instruments within institutions, technologies, and inherited narratives. Volume I records not the triumphant completion of a theory, but the way a concern grew out of concrete questions and began to submit itself to the test of reality.

Author's Note

These essays did not begin with a finished theory in search of examples. They began with questions from friends: Why does a person become afraid? Why does a technology inspire both excitement and unease? Why does an institution turn people into instruments in the name of protecting them? The writing often came before the answer, and judgments changed in the face of new facts and counterexamples. In gathering these pieces into a book, I have not tried to erase the particular circumstances in which they arose. I hope, instead, that the questions will remain visible after the news has receded. Readers need not accept every conclusion here. If these essays can help a question be asked more precisely, they will already have done part of their work.

Translator's Note

The Chinese and English editions of this book are published separately, but their volumes, parts, essay numbers, and hidden stable source IDs correspond exactly. The translation is based on the edited Chinese manuscript, not on a line-by-line transcription of the original X posts. Platform residue, passing references, and conversational phrasing that depended entirely on the moment of posting were addressed during the Chinese editing process. The English text preserves the historical setting of each essay while adding only the minimum context needed by readers who may not share the same news memory.

Self-as-an-End (SAE) remains in English throughout the book. It is not a synonym for self-interest, self-actualization, or autonomy. The term extends a Kantian concern with persons as ends in themselves into a broader question: whether a person is recognized, within the institutions and social structures that shape their life, as the ultimate source of the ends they live by rather than as a means for goals imposed by an external system. Where the distinction matters, the translation separates Kant's formulation from the author's expanded use of Self-as-an-End without adding an apparatus of scholarly notes.

Core terms are kept consistent. Instrumentalization and self-instrumentalization retain distinct meanings, while terms such as alienation, agency, and subjectivity are distinguished according to context. Consistency, however, does not mean mirroring Chinese syntax sentence by sentence. The translation aims to preserve the force of the judgments, the humor, the hesitation, and the author's habit of correcting himself. Sentence length and paragraphing have sometimes been adjusted for natural English, but no new argument has been added.

Chinese institutions, classical texts, internet culture, films, television series, and games are explained only where comprehension requires it. Personal names follow the Romanized forms commonly used by the people concerned, and titles are kept consistent after their first appearance. Relative dates have been anchored to the historical moment of composition, and predictions remain predictions rather than being rewritten in light of later events. References already present in the essays remain part of the prose; no new system of citations or notes has been imposed.

Some wordplay, kinship-inflected forms of address, and political resonances cannot be carried over at equal weight. In such cases, the translation favors the function the expression serves in the argument, with a brief explanation in the text only when indispensable. Translation is itself an act of interpretation. The aim here is not to remove every ambiguity on the author's behalf, but to let English readers encounter the same questions that confront readers of the Chinese original.

General Preface

Writing as a Form of Prayer

I could hear the friendly teasing in the question, but I also thought it was a good one, and more meaningful than it first appeared.

Kafka, yes, the Kafka who wrote The Metamorphosis, once said that writing was a form of prayer. I agree. First, why prayer rather than some other metaphor? Sincerity, passion, and persistence can all describe serious writing, but they describe so many things that they can be made to fit almost anything. Prayer is different. It has a very specific structure: we speak to someone or something that is under no obligation to answer us.

Every part of that sentence matters. We are speaking, but we are not merely talking to ourselves. Our words have a direction. They are addressed to someone. Yet that person owes us no reply. However precisely, sincerely, or forcefully we speak, we cannot demand an answer.

This is where a writer stands. We can take responsibility only for our half, for putting the words into the world. Whether understanding occurs belongs to another person, and it may not occur at all. We cannot complete the process by ourselves.

Once that structure becomes clear, several other things come into focus.

The first is that writing asks us not to close a question too quickly.

Imagine someone writing, “I have so much, and yet I am still unhappy.” They could immediately add, “So I must be ungrateful.” The essay would be over, and the question would have been shut down before it had really begun. Or they could keep writing: What exactly do I mean by unhappy? Can I value what I have and still be unwilling to continue living under a particular arrangement? Why have the conditions other people consider sufficient failed to address what is actually troubling me?

Here, writing is not simply a way to express emotion. It keeps a ready-made answer from replacing the real question too soon. Writing resembles prayer because the writer does not have to play the role of someone who has already figured everything out. The writer can admit, “I do not yet understand, but I am willing to look carefully.”

Flannery O’Connor’s A Prayer Journal is a good example, and a genuinely religious one. She asks to become a better writer and to have her work published, but she also examines her own hunger for success. She prays for clarity and asks whether her desires might be obscuring what she is trying to understand. She does not reduce prayer to “Please give me what I want.”

From this we can draw a way of writing that does not depend on religious faith: I hope for an answer, but I am also willing to discover that what needs to change may be not only the answer, but the way I ask the question.

The second is that writing not only asks the world to listen to me. It also allows the world to correct me.

If an essay’s only task is to prove that its author was right all along, then the evidence, the people in the essay, and even the reader will all be arranged as tools in support of the conclusion. Another kind of writing allows what resists us to remain on the page. We may love a beautiful sentence and delete it when we discover that it is inaccurate. We may begin by casting a person as the embodiment of an idea, only to find that the particulars of their life refuse the label. We may think the argument is complete, then return to rewrite it when a reader points out a counterexample.

In such moments, writing does more than amplify the voice of the “I.” It also limits the “I’s” power to explain everything however it pleases. Writers sometimes describe revision this way in interviews: at a certain point, a sentence is no longer merely something they own; it begins to answer back. This is a description of the experience of writing, not a claim that inspiration comes from some supernatural force.

Of course, being willing to be corrected does not mean abandoning judgment. We still have to decide whether a correction deserves to be accepted or is merely another untested demand. Openness does not mean accepting everything indiscriminately.

The third is that writing longs for a response, but cannot turn that response into a command.

We can make a sentence more precise, describe an experience more concretely, and strengthen an argument. None of that guarantees that another person will understand, much less that they must agree. To write for readers we do not yet know therefore involves an unguaranteed trust: perhaps someone else is facing a similar confusion; perhaps they will pause over a particular sentence; perhaps they will disagree, but in doing so help me see what I could not see on my own.

Nor must a response always come from someone else. A private journal may give one’s future self a chance to look back and understand one’s present self more fairly.

Sometimes a lack of response is a real disappointment, but it does not cancel the value of having spoken. At the same time, writing seriously does not entitle me to anyone’s attention. I hope to reach you, but I do not claim ownership over your response. I believe communication is worth attempting, but I do not assume it must succeed.

This metaphor also reveals something else: prayer done badly and writing done badly fail in almost exactly the same ways.

One failure turns prayer into a demand that says nothing but “Give me this.” Its equivalent in writing is a sentence that is always asking for something: agreement, sympathy, recognition. Another form of prayer is performed for an audience, with posture more important than substance. Its equivalent is writing designed mainly to look profound. A third begins with the answer already settled and merely recites it. Its equivalent is an essay whose conclusion is fixed in the first paragraph and whose middle merely builds a road to it. All three are still forms of speech, but the structure in which “the other person owes me no answer” has disappeared. They do not actually need a response. They need only a witness.

Prayer is also often imagined as waiting for some power to take care of things on our behalf. In this metaphor, the opposite is true.

We cannot guarantee that a neglected experience will eventually be understood fairly, but we can begin by writing about it without distorting it further. We cannot guarantee that everyone will treat others with seriousness, but we can refuse to reduce the people in our writing to props that prove our point. We cannot guarantee that our theory is correct, but we can state its premises clearly, preserve the objections, and mark what remains unresolved.

A wish does not have to remain only a wish simply because its fulfillment is not guaranteed. It can enter into very concrete acts: how a sentence is written, how an example is handled, whether a revision is honest.

For the same reason, this kind of “prayer” need not be gentle or serene. It can contain anger. It can protest harm. It can also express gratitude for something good. Writing down an ordinary moment with one’s family need not serve any conclusion. It may simply recognize that the moment deserves to be seen, that our busyness should not make it disappear merely because it was not spectacular. In such moments, writing is not asking life for more. It is responding to the life that has already happened.

Finally, prayer may explain why we write with such seriousness, but it cannot serve as evidence that what we have written is true. An essay may begin in pain, hope, or care, but its argument must still withstand scrutiny. Sincerity does not make a conclusion true, and an explanation does not become valid merely because it comforts us. Conversely, the willingness to delete a claim that offers great comfort but cannot survive examination may be the clearest expression of a writer’s honesty.

Of course, it is also a mistake to sanctify writing into a mission one is never allowed to stop. Writing does not require us to suffer continuously, to produce words every day, or to turn every experience into a work.

What moves me most about the metaphor, then, is that it places limitation and hope in the same sentence: I cannot guarantee understanding, yet I remain willing to understand; I cannot guarantee that I will be heard, yet I remain willing to speak carefully; I cannot guarantee that I am right, yet I am willing to let what I write receive an answer.

It does not say, “May the world prove me right.” It says: may I be able to see what does not conform to my wishes and still write about it honestly; may these words reach others without prescribing their answers for them.

Volume I: From Instrumentalization to Self-as-an-End

Volume I begins with judgment. Markets, artificial intelligence, institutions, platforms, and popular culture may seem to belong to different worlds, yet each repeatedly asks the same question: as our tools grow more powerful, are we handing over our judgment as well? These essays appear in the order in which they were written because the central idea did not arrive all at once. It first hid inside questions of risk, trust, narrative, and self-doubt. Only later did it acquire the name “Self-as-an-End.” Volume I records not the triumphant completion of a theory, but the way a concern grew out of concrete questions and then began, in turn, to submit itself to the test of reality.

Part I: Learning to Judge

Judgment is not the same as guessing an outcome correctly. A prediction may come true by accident, and a system may work for a long time without being able to bear the burden of choice for each person within it. The essays in this part enter an uncertain world through markets, technology, law, and education. They ask why we trust one signal rather than another, and how we can identify what we have failed to see. Tools can expand information, computation, and action, but they cannot answer, on behalf of the people who use them, what is worth doing. The easier it becomes to hand a decision to a model, platform, or authority, the more important it is to retain responsibility for judgment.

1. Can AI Predict Markets?

Using AI models to predict markets is possible to a certain extent, but only if we understand both their strengths and their limits.

1/ Feasibility and Advantages

1.1/ Powerful Pattern Recognition

AI, especially deep-learning and time-series models, can capture complex nonlinear relationships that people may miss, such as interactions among price movements, macroeconomic factors, news sentiment, and on-chain data.

1.2/ Richer Data Dimensions

Traditional quantitative strategies may rely only on price and trading volume. AI models can integrate many more kinds of data: news, posts on X, Google Trends, macroeconomic indicators, corporate earnings reports, and on-chain activity.

Large models offer advantages here that earlier models could not match.

1.3/ Well Suited to Short-Term Forecasting and Trading Support

In high-frequency or short-horizon trading, AI can gain an advantage by quickly identifying small signals, such as order-book dynamics and arbitrage opportunities.

2/ Limits and Risks

2.1/ Unavoidable “Noise”

Financial markets are highly random, and signals are difficult to separate from noise. However powerful AI becomes, it cannot guarantee consistent excess returns.

2.2/ Overfitting

An AI model may perform extremely well on historical data, then fail when the market’s structure changes. This is especially common in financial markets when so-called black swan events occur.

Of course, almost every model is vulnerable to overfitting, so this is not necessarily a problem unique to current AI models.

2.3/ Feedback Effects

If many people use similar AI models, their predictions may be priced in early, causing the models to stop working. Capital may also pile into the same position, creating the risk of a market crash.

Again, this is a limitation of finance itself rather than of AI alone.

2.4/ Lack of Causal Explanation

AI is better at predicting correlations than explaining causation. That can create problems for risk control and regulatory compliance.

For current models, this limitation is particularly serious.

3/ Practical Applications

Quantitative hedge funds such as Citadel, Two Sigma, and Renaissance already make extensive use of AI models, particularly in short-term trading and risk management.

Retail investors can also use AI, for example to analyze news sentiment or forecast ETF fund flows, to improve their decisions. But they cannot treat it as a tool that guarantees profits without losses.

AI is better suited to assisting decisions than making them independently. It can produce signals, while people combine those signals with macroeconomic and fundamental analysis before making a judgment.

Conclusion

AI models can make investing and trading more efficient and accurate, but they cannot eliminate market uncertainty.

The most prudent approach is to use AI as a tool that augments decision-making, not as a crystal ball that replaces human judgment.

The constructive direction for further thought is clear: trading strategies that use AI will have an advantage over strategies that do not.

2. Can Luck Make High Leverage Sustainable?

The best answer is that we still do not fully appreciate how lethal a black swan event can be to a highly leveraged position.

1/ Leverage Amplifies Every Movement

Leverage means using a small amount of one’s own capital to control a much larger position.

Under ordinary market fluctuations, it can multiply gains. But when an extreme event occurs, it multiplies losses as well.

At five-to-one leverage, for example, a 20 percent decline is enough to wipe out the original capital.

2/ Black Swan Events Often Move in Jumps

Black swans often arrive with sudden news: war, regulatory bans, major hacks, financial crises, and so on.

The resulting shock is not a smooth, linear decline. Prices can gap sharply downward, leaving stop-loss orders no time to execute.

A leveraged position can blow straight through its margin, be liquidated instantly, and even leave the trader in debt.

3/ Vanishing Liquidity and Cascading Liquidations

When a black swan arrives, market liquidity can dry up. Buyers disappear while sell orders pile up.

Highly leveraged positions are forcibly liquidated, causing automatic selling that drives prices down further. The decline triggers still more liquidations, which produce still more selling, until the market enters a downward spiral.

Leveraged traders are often the first fuel fed into this chain reaction.

4/ The Risk Cannot Be Fully Hedged in Advance

Ordinary risks can be partly reduced through risk controls, stop losses, and hedging.

Black swans, by definition, cannot be predicted or priced in ahead of time. A leveraged account therefore has almost no room for defense.

Even attempts to buy insurance through options or hedged positions may fail under extreme conditions, or the necessary liquidity may disappear.

5/ Psychology and Capital Management Break Down

The more leverage a trader uses, the greater the psychological pressure. In a black swan market, fear and greed can make traders miss their chance to escape.

Investors with low or no leverage at least retain the possibility of surviving and waiting for a recovery. High leverage can end the game in a single stroke.

Conclusion

Black swan events bring extreme price movements that cannot be predicted or controlled. Leverage magnifies that uncontrollable risk to the point of forced liquidation, which is why it can be fatal to highly leveraged traders.

3. If Anyone Can Buy US Stocks, Why Do Investment Barriers Persist?

The issue is really one of asset classes. Every asset class has a different average long-term compound annual growth rate, or CAGR. From that perspective, publicly traded US stocks are not even the best-performing category.

Roughly speaking, long-term CAGRs rank from highest to lowest as follows: startup equity, venture capital, private equity, US public equities, real estate, government bonds, and cash.

Cash comes last. That is easy to understand. Its CAGR is only 2 to 3 percent in interest, roughly the rate of inflation. Its real purchasing power barely grows over the long term and may decline during periods of high inflation, as it was doing in the United States when this essay was written. Cash is useful mainly for liquidity and protection from volatility, not appreciation. Ordinary people can, of course, hold it freely.

Government bonds, such as ten-year US Treasuries, do somewhat better at roughly 4 to 5 percent. They are low-volatility assets used mainly to hedge risk. Buying Treasury bonds directly can be difficult for ordinary investors, but Treasury ETFs offer indirect access with almost no barrier to entry.

Without leverage, real estate has a long-term CAGR of only 6 to 8 percent. Its real appreciation is not especially high. Most of its returns come from leverage, rental cash flow, and tax advantages. Here, ordinary investors begin to face serious barriers. Down payments are high, and high interest rates also reduce returns. REIT ETFs provide some access, but once fund fees are included, ordinary investors still find it difficult to capture the asset class’s true return.

Including reinvested dividends, US public equities have a CAGR of roughly 9 to 10 percent. The S&P 500, for example, has a long-term nominal CAGR of approximately 9.5 to 10 percent. This is the highest-returning asset class that ordinary people can enter conveniently.

Beyond it lie assets with still higher returns, but ordinary people are excluded by design.

After management fees, private equity has a long-term average CAGR of roughly 10 to 13 percent. Top funds may earn more, though survivorship bias is substantial. Its returns derive mainly from high leverage, the illiquidity premium, and financial management. The best funds are designed for family offices and institutions, and their thresholds are high. Ordinary people not only cannot participate; they often do not even know such opportunities exist. We frequently hear someone say that a company is excellent but, unfortunately, not yet public, and that they will buy it as soon as it lists.

Venture capital, or VC, can average about 15 percent overall, while top funds may reach 20 percent or more. Here, ordinary people have almost no opportunity at all. Everyone may know that a16z or Sequoia is formidable, but how many ordinary investors can become limited partners in their funds?

Startup equity through angel investing may be the best-performing category of all, with a theoretical average of 20 to 30 percent or more. But its variance is enormous. Around 90 percent of angel investments return close to zero, while a small number of hundredfold gains determine the entire portfolio’s performance. Success demands considerable skill from the angel investor, and ordinary people again have almost no access.

The assets that truly widen intergenerational wealth gaps, then, are private equity before an IPO, limited-partner interests in top venture funds, and startup equity with advantageous return distributions, such as angel and early-round investments. Ordinary people struggle even to learn that these markets are open to anyone at all.

This also helps explain the popularity of crypto assets, especially Bitcoin. Their historical CAGR from 2011 to 2024 was extremely high, above 50 percent, and anyone could participate. Whether that rate can be extrapolated is another matter. Crypto’s true long-term CAGR remains to be seen.

4. If Crypto Is Centralizing, Why Is Self-Custody Still Growing?

This is an excellent question. These two trends are not really strengthening decentralization. They are institutional repairs for the risks created by cash-like private keys.

Hardware wallets such as Ledger and Trezor have become popular, while multisignature self-custody through systems such as Safe and Squads has been widely adopted. DAOs, venture funds, and high-net-worth users now rarely rely on a single private key.

On the surface, this looks like a sign that users are becoming more security-conscious and that self-custody is maturing. The deeper reason is that the single-key model is unsustainable in the real world. People are using institutions to repair cryptography.

A hardware wallet is not decentralization. It is a physical safe for a private key. It addresses the attack surface, not the relationship of trust. Its purpose is to move the key from a general-purpose device connected to the internet, such as a phone or computer, into a closed, single-purpose device.

This is like moving cash from your pocket into a safe. The system has not become more decentralized; it has only become more resistant to attack. The user remains the sole bearer. Losing the device and seed phrase can still mean losing the assets forever. A coerced signature, the so-called wrench attack, can still transfer the assets away. The risks have not disappeared, and responsibility has not been distributed. Only the form of the risk has changed.

Hardware wallets are therefore best suited to technically skilled users, long-term cold storage, and people who can bear full responsibility for failure. Their core value is protection from hackers, not protection from predictable human frailty.

Multisignature wallets mark the true institutional turning point, and their significance is much greater than that of hardware wallets. Multisignature custody introduces governance rather than stronger technology. It does not depend on more advanced cryptography. Instead, it divides control, requires consensus, and deliberately adds friction. That logic closely resembles the foundations of modern finance.

Multisignature custody is like requiring a board of directors to sign off. It is a quasi-institutional form of self-custody. In practice, it is used mainly to protect against insiders, a founder as a single point of failure, coercion, and impulsive decisions. Unlike a hardware wallet, its core value is to counter predictable human failure, not hackers.

Multisignature custody does not eliminate trust. It makes trust explicit. With a single key, we must place 100 percent of our trust in one person. With multiple signatures, we can specify whom to trust, how much to trust them, and who has a veto. This is a shift from implicit trust to structured trust.

Why are both approaches rising at once? Because they repair two different kinds of failure. Hardware wallets address technical attacks; multisignature custody addresses organizational risk.

Both are valuable. Unfortunately, neither solves the problem of adoption at the scale of ordinary users.

Consider the history of cash: people moved from holding cash themselves, to storing it in private safes, to using corporate accounts, and finally to the banking system.

Crypto is following almost the same path, only faster. It began with single-signature self-custody, moved to hardware wallets as the ultimate personal defense, then to multisignature custody as the starting point of organization, and finally to custodianship as the basis of social scale.

What can carry capital at scale is not a “safer private key,” but an accountable structure of control. A systematized, centralized, and compliant structure will inevitably emerge to bring blockchain technology to broad, mass adoption.

5. Why Is Privacy Crypto’s Next Frontier?

This question is closely related to mass adoption. The renewed interest in privacy does not mean that crypto has suddenly returned to idealism. It means that crypto is leaving its frontier stage and entering an institutional one. Privacy is not meant to evade regulation. It is what will allow crypto assets to enter mainstream finance and the real economy.

Without privacy, institutions will not move on-chain, businesses will not use blockchain systems, and high-net-worth investors will not hold crypto assets at scale.

Blockchain’s current design is counterintuitive. By default, Bitcoin and Ethereum make every transaction permanently public. Every address is traceable, and asset flows can be mapped transparently. In the early years, this was an advantage. It made verification possible and constrained abuse by centralized actors. In a financialized system, however, the same feature becomes a fatal flaw.

In traditional finance, privacy is built into the structure. Account balances are not publicly visible. Counterparties are not disclosed. Portfolio holdings are reported only to regulators. Much of a firm’s activity is treated as a trade secret.

In today’s on-chain world, if I send you $100,000, the entire world is told about it. That is not financial activity. It is a ledger broadcasting itself in public.

Privacy becomes important as blockchain users shift from technical enthusiasts to businesses and eventually to nation-scale institutions. During blockchain’s first decade, its users were geeks and early speculators with relatively little money, a high tolerance for risk, and little concern about exposure. Some even took pride in transparency. They did not need privacy.

In the years leading up to this essay, institutions and businesses began adopting blockchain. Hedge funds, market makers, family offices, and corporate treasuries all care intensely about preventing others from targeting their positions, front-running their trades, copying their strategies, or analyzing their finances. A system of complete transparency with no privacy is unacceptable to them.

Future applications at the level of the state will require even more information to be hidden from the public while remaining visible to regulators. Taxation, social security, cross-border settlement, and government subsidies are all examples. A state does not need “anonymity.” It needs rules about what is hidden from whom and what is visible to whom. That is the heart of compliant privacy.

Regulators do not actually need total transparency. They need the ability to look through the system when necessary. Their real concerns are anti-money-laundering rules, terrorist financing, accountable taxation, asset freezes, and enforceability. In other words, regulators need selective visibility, not a world in which everyone is forced to stand naked in public.

The privacy technologies under discussion therefore include zero-knowledge proofs, selective disclosure, view keys, and compliance layers.

Why is privacy also a matter of safety? On a fully transparent chain, criminals can identify their targets precisely. We have compared private keys to cash. Publishing an on-chain balance is therefore like publishing the exact amount of cash stored in one’s home, something no one would accept in the physical world.

Hacking, extortion, and kidnapping had already become very real problems in crypto when this essay was written. Large on-chain addresses were continually targeted by phishing attacks. High-net-worth crypto users faced targeted assaults. DAO treasuries were monitored over long periods. Privacy does not exist to help bad people do bad things. It exists so that good people are not so easy for bad actors to find.

Why has privacy matured only now? Because the technology has finally become usable. In the past, zero-knowledge computation was expensive, user experience was abysmal, wallets lacked support, audits were difficult, and regulators were hostile. The maturation of ZK-SNARK and STARK technology, account abstraction, modular chains, a narrative of compliant privacy, and AI-assisted zero-knowledge verification has begun turning a sound theory into a workable engineering system.

The privacy at issue here is therefore not the strong anonymity and regulatory resistance associated with projects such as Monero and Zcash. What we should expect is a new generation of privacy technology that can be audited, supports selective disclosure, and works with regulators. That is the mainstream path for the next decade.

The visible future is not a binary choice between making every transaction public and making every transaction completely anonymous. Rules will determine who can see what. Such privacy technology is, at its core, financial infrastructure that combines access control with cryptographic proof.

6. Can Large Language Models Beat Humans in Prediction Markets?

I agree that large language models are not good at trading stocks or crypto. My doctoral dissertation reached that very conclusion. But they are extremely well suited to analyzing prediction markets, where they can possess a real edge.

Stocks and crypto are poor fits because their prices emerge from real-time, high-frequency competition dominated by noise.

Prediction-market questions, by contrast, require semantic judgments of probability rather than contests of execution speed. Their usefulness depends more on the quality of the data and the depth of the model.

A yes-or-no question is a discrete event with explicit settlement conditions. It contains much less noise than a stock or crypto price, and its logic is easier to explain. Large language models are not good at timing, but they are very good at judgment. This is where they fit.

The source of alpha in prediction markets is a gap in understanding. Most retail participants lag behind the narrative, become excessively emotional, or engage in wishful thinking. These are precisely the areas in which large language models can excel: rapidly reading real-time data, remaining calm, and avoiding personal preferences.

Some people argue that the market price must always be more accurate than a large language model. But a prediction-market price is not more accurate at every moment. At any given time, the price is simply the consensus on which the current participants are willing to wager. That consensus may contain a collective bias.

This leaves room for large language models. When the present consensus diverges from what can actually occur in the next moment, a model can provide an edge.

The market may underestimate a legal procedure, misunderstand a timeline, or reduce a political contest to an emotional story.

Most important, a large language model’s forecast does not itself change the prediction market’s ultimate outcome, apart from illegal conduct such as insider manipulation of timing. The forecast therefore lacks the reflexivity of a financial-market prediction. Trading behavior does not alter how the real-world event is settled.

This also explains why AI companies focused on prediction markets were beginning to proliferate.

Prediction markets are evolving from speculative entertainment into a society-wide infrastructure for probability.

The ecosystem around Polymarket, for example, had already begun producing AI probability forecasters, AI pipelines that turn news into probabilities, and systems that detect market inefficiencies.

If I were beginning a PhD today, I would change direction and specialize in AI and prediction markets. I might even start a company directly, instead of going to work for a large technology firm.

7. How Does International Law Address a Regime That Massacres Its Own People?

With tensions in Iran at the time, this was a painful question to consider. Let us set emotion aside as far as possible and ask only how international law “handles” such a case.

International law explicitly prohibits a regime from massacring people regardless of their nationality, whether they are foreigners or its own citizens. The unsettling question is not whether the law applies, but how it applies: who enforces it, when, and whether enforcement is actually possible.

The postwar consensus in international law is that nationality cannot make a massacre lawful. A state that kills its own citizens on a mass scale is still committing an international crime. “The people I killed were violent troublemakers from my own country” is not a valid legal defense.

The first relevant instrument is the 1948 Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide. Its central principle is that genocide is an international crime in both peace and war.

The treaty establishes that the victims may be a state’s own nationals and that the perpetrator may be their own government. Neither sovereign immunity nor the priority of domestic law is recognized as a defense.

In plain language, a regime that kills its own people can still be guilty of genocide.

The second category is crimes against humanity, an offense in international criminal law designed specifically to address violence by a state against a population.

Its elements include a widespread or systematic attack against civilians, including killing, cleansing, enforced disappearance, and torture, without regard to nationality.

Historically, this category was created to answer a particular question: if a state lawfully controls its military, police, and courts, does that mean no one can hold it responsible when it kills people?

The answer is no. International law can.

That sounds reassuring. There is law to apply. But who hears the case?

One answer is the International Criminal Court. The ICC principally tries genocide, crimes against humanity, and war crimes.

It prosecutes individuals, not states. Those individuals may include heads of state and senior military or political officials.

This is where reality intervenes. The ICC’s decision to act does not automatically produce an enforceable result.

Whether international law can actually “handle” the crime depends not only on law and adjudication, but above all on a structure of enforcement.

International law has no international police force. In practice, enforcement usually requires authorization by the United Nations Security Council, cooperation from national judicial authorities, or accountability after military defeat or a change of regime.

If a regime remains stable, enjoys the protection of a great power, or benefits from a Security Council veto, the law may exist without being enforced. The outcome still depends on a contest of political power.

If the regime does not fall, is not defeated militarily, and faces no domestic reckoning, accountability is nearly impossible.

During Augusto Pinochet’s military rule in Chile, for example, the state carried out systematic repression, yet he was not tried in his own country for many years. He was briefly detained in Britain late in life but never finally convicted, probably in part because of support from the United States.

International law can therefore resolve the question of legitimacy. It cannot resolve the question of power. A massacre is always unlawful, but whether anyone is held accountable ultimately depends on whether power changes hands.

8. How Can Adults Reeducate Themselves with AI?

This is a good question because it asks about reeducation, not retraining.

In the age of AI, the greatest risk for adults who are not AI-native, which means people like us, is not that we will fail to use the new tools. It is that we will use them with the cognitive operating system of the old world.

Before discussing methods, we need to ask which underlying assumptions have changed.

First, knowledge has moved beyond even the internet era. It is no longer scarce, and both true and false information are more abundant than ever. We should stop treating possession of information as an identity asset and make verification central instead.

Second, thought no longer has to proceed along a single synchronous thread. It can become asynchronous and parallel. We can take one step in an argument, ask AI to conduct deeper research, and return when the results are ready.

Finally, our most important value now lies in judgment, as perhaps it always should have. AI makes output extremely cheap. It can produce a mountain of repetitive prose. What remains scarce is knowing what should not be done, what is an illusion, and what may be right in principle but wrong in timing. Reeducation is, at its core, the retraining of judgment.

Once those premises are clear, what can we do in practice?

First, we can force AI to argue with us. Instead of asking, “Help me write X,” ask, “Make the strongest possible case against this idea.” Invite criticism with questions such as, “Assume I am wrong. Where is the error?” This trains us to distrust our own reasoning in a useful way.

Second, ask AI to analyze the hidden premises in our thinking. Our greatest problem is often not a lack of depth, but the number of premises we have left unstated. Give AI a judgment and ask it to identify the assumptions and implied causal links. We may discover that what felt like intuition was really outdated experience.

Third, use AI to analyze the reasoning rather than jumping straight to a conclusion. Replace “Will X succeed?” with “What conditions would have to hold for X to succeed?” Ask, “Which constraints are hard, and which are soft?” The point is not to obtain a conclusion. It is to have AI divide the question into subproblems and turn a binary yes-or-no dispute into a question of probability. Once again, prediction markets deserve some praise.

Fourth, ask AI to broaden our perspective. Everyone tends to view the present through the experience of their youth. As we grow older, we need help understanding how younger people see the world. Ask, “How would Gen Z or Gen Alpha view this?” Or, “Would young people hear this and think I am an out-of-touch old-timer?”

Finally, we can use AI to record how we made a decision so that we can review it later. Ask, “After this discussion, why did I reach this judgment today?” Six months later, return and ask, “Were my assumptions about the world correct?” and “What did I overlook?”

For adults who are not AI-native, the key to reeducation is not learning new material. It is systematically clearing out bad models.

Taking courses and collecting certificates belong to an older era. In the AI age, what matters is problem-driven inquiry, brainstorming with AI, rapid verification, and frequent correction.

Put simply, we do not need to “learn AI.” We need to force AI to show us what we must change. That can be painful, but once again: no pain, no gain.

Reeducation in the AI age is not comfortable. It destroys old professional certainties. It shows us that we have been trying to navigate a new continent with an old map. It forces us to admit that experience matters less than judgment.

The good news is that adults who complete this cognitive upgrade often gain an advantage over AI-native generations. We have lived through more than one cycle. We understand institutions, incentives, and structures of power more deeply. Add AI as a GPU for the brain, and it is like giving wings to a tiger. It feels almost like a cheat code.

9. Why Must Engineers Learn to Argue with AI?

The answer is emphatically yes. Engineers should argue with AI, and not only over small things. The ability to conduct a serious argument with AI is itself an engineering skill.

An engineer who cannot argue with AI has effectively surrendered all judgment to it.

An engineer who can argue with AI is using it to extend the upper limit of their own engineering ability.

Why the conflict? Because AI is naturally capable of making things up, and it does so in a way that is structurally complete, confidently expressed, and apparently implementable. Engineers do not fear code that is obviously wrong. They fear code that looks right from every angle. In engineering, that can be fatal.

If no one challenges the model, its code may compile and pass every test. Each local component may look correct, while the system as a whole is fundamentally wrong.

An engineer’s natural responsibility, then, is to become the leader who does not trust AI.

Most engineering failures do not occur because someone typed the code incorrectly. They begin because the design itself was mistaken. The incidents we encounter most often arise from false architectural assumptions, ignored edge cases, or misunderstood use cases.

AI can easily reproduce our existing false premises and magnify them into a plan that looks impressively professional.

Calling this an “argument” may sound emotional. In substance, it is a risk scan. Challenging AI forces it to expose our bad assumptions, identify uncertainties, and list possible paths to failure. This is a question of engineering safety.

So what should engineers argue about, and how?

Ask: “Which premises does this solution depend on?” “Which of them are most likely to fail in a real production environment?” “At what input scale does the system break?” “What form will the failure take?”

Also ask: “If this component fails, what is the worst possible consequence?” “Could it fail silently?” “Could it create cascading risk?”

These are the questions a senior engineer already asks while reviewing a junior engineer’s code. Now we must ask them of AI.

We can also challenge the model from different professional roles. As a product manager, ask, “Can this system survive a tenfold increase in scale six months from now?” As a site reliability engineer, ask, “Is this design stable? Will it develop problems after running for a long time?” As a security engineer, ask, “How could a hacker compromise it?” As legal counsel, ask, “Is it compliant?” As a finance lead, ask, “Can we reduce the cost?”

Must the argument continue forever? When can we stop pushing back?

We have done enough when we can clearly explain why we chose the solution. At that point, even without AI, we would have designed something much like it. The thinking process is complete.

As with students using AI, the technology will infinitely widen the gap between engineers who merely execute and engineers who exercise judgment. If an engineer simply writes whatever AI says to write, what is that engineer for? Sooner or later, they will be fired. An engineer who uses AI to expose their own potential mistakes more quickly may become the legendary 10x engineer.

10. Why Are MAGA and the Far Left Growing Further Apart?

My observation is that the two groups share a surprising amount in their emotional and psychological structure.

On the surface, one chants “Make America Great Again” while the other denounces systemic oppression. They regard each other with hostility and appear irreconcilable. Remove the identity labels, however, and the sources of their anger overlap considerably. More and more people feel that effort no longer leads reliably to opportunity.

The Red MAGA narrative says: I followed the rules, worked, paid taxes, and supported my family, yet globalization and cultural change pushed me to the margins.

The far-left narrative says: the system was never designed for most people in the first place. Both the starting point and the rewards have already been fixed.

The two sides speak different languages and blame different targets, but both begin from the same experience: the traditional social path is losing the certainty it once provided.

This is not simply a problem of wealth inequality. Societies have historically endured inequality, cultural conflict, and political disagreement. The crucial condition is that most people believe tomorrow will be better than today. Once that belief begins to collapse, anger is no longer an aberration. It becomes an understandable response.

Curiously, cultural and identity issues enter public discussion more easily than structural economic problems. They are more visible, more emotionally charged, and easier to digest. Anger therefore tends to appear as “left versus right” or “progressive versus conservative.” The more intense the dispute becomes, the easier it is to postpone the genuinely difficult problems.

Red MAGA and the far left share distrust of the existing system, estrangement from elites, an uncertain view of the future, and a sense that opportunities they should have had were taken from them. Their difference is directional. One looks to the past for dignity; the other demands justice from the future.

The question worth asking, then, is not which side to join. It is whether the system can still offer most people a real and attainable path upward in return for their effort. If that question remains unanswered, every political narrative will eventually become an emotional outlet rather than a solution.

Is there a solution? Yes. That is a question for the next essay.

11. Why Does Silicon Valley Venture Capital Meet at Blue Bottle?

It is not only Blue Bottle. Plenty of other coffee shops serve the same purpose. But Blue Bottle is certainly the most culturally representative.

The reason, I suspect, is that coffee is the lowest-commitment setting for a meeting.

In Silicon Valley, an invitation to Blue Bottle carries an unspoken message: I am willing to give you twenty minutes, but I promise nothing more. It is less formal than an office and less time-consuming than a meal. One can arrive quickly and leave gracefully, without emotional weight. This is an exploratory contact, not a committed relationship.

The locations matter too. Meetings often take place on Sand Hill Road or in Palo Alto, SoMa, and Hayes Valley, usually downstairs from venture firms. A VC can see three people in an hour and hear four pitches over a single coffee. The calendar can be compressed to its limit. It is essentially a physical LinkedIn feed.

Blue Bottle’s spatial design supports this function. The tables are small, there are no private rooms, the environment is a little noisy, and the setting discourages long conversations. It tells you that this is not a place for performing importance or speaking in empty abstractions. As a founder, you have not yet been “invited into the office.” You are sitting across a table from the investor as an equal, which also suits Silicon Valley’s anti-hierarchical story about itself.

The coffee is therefore the first filter. The VC is watching whether you can explain yourself in five minutes, whether you know when to stop, and whether you can remain composed in a setting where no one is treating you as especially important. If the conversation goes well, you come to the office the following week. If it is merely fine, the meeting ends with “Great to meet you.”

Blue Bottle makes good coffee, but its prices remain approachable. It feels elegant without becoming too important. Its very unimportance means that no one owes anyone anything, judgments can be more honest, and the power differential is compressed as far as possible.

The coffee, then, is not the point. The VC wants to decide quickly whether the relationship is worth continuing without committing to any relationship at all. Does that sound like a five-minute speed date? More or less.

12. If People Know the Female Streamer Is AI, Why Do They Still Pay?

One rarely mentioned reason is that men understand what other men want better than women do.

The paying audience for these livestreams is predominantly male. What they consume is not a real person but an emotional service: the experience of being affirmed, listened to, treated gently, and accepted without judgment.

A male operator often knows more precisely than a real woman when to act coy, when to offer praise, and when to seem just out of reach. He is a user himself. He knows exactly where the pain point is.

A real woman faces natural obstacles in performing this service. She may have emotional fluctuations of her own. She has boundaries. She may instinctively feel, “This is too much.” A male operator experiences none of that psychological friction. He is simply operating an interface with a clearly defined objective.

The combination of a male operator and a female skin succeeds not merely because it is technically easy to build, but because it is the most efficient design for emotional service.

At a deeper level, the audience has not really been deceived. Many viewers vaguely know that the persona is fake and simply do not care. They are not buying authenticity. They are buying stability: endless patience, endless gentleness, and a companion who never refuses an ill-timed emotional demand. A real person cannot sustain those qualities indefinitely.

This is the inevitable result of disassembling intimacy into functional modules. Whether the woman is truly a woman matters less and less. Perhaps one day, whether she is even human will not matter either.

13. Where Does the East-West Divide in Venture Capital Come From?

The difference begins with two judgments about how much an idea is worth. The Eastern intuition is that once someone hears my idea, they may execute it before I do.

That intuition assumes the idea itself has substantial, reproducible value, while the threshold for execution is relatively low. The decisive advantage goes to whoever learns the information first. Secrecy therefore becomes a competitive advantage, and information itself becomes a resource. Historically, this assumption has often held in China, Japan, and South Korea, where imitation was cheap, legal protection was weak, and first-mover advantages could be erased quickly.

Silicon Valley begins with the opposite intuition. If hearing one sentence from me is enough for you to destroy my company, the idea was never truly mine. In Silicon Valley, 99 percent of ideas are considered “obvious.” The hard work lies in making sound judgments repeatedly, hiring, raising capital, enduring pressure, and revising the plan again and again under uncertainty. The default assumption is that the idea contributes 1 percent and the founding team’s execution contributes 99 percent.

The two cultures therefore fear entirely different things. In the East, a time advantage plus imitation may allow someone else to overtake you, so the rational choice is not to speak, to speak later, or to tell only a tiny circle. Silicon Valley fears that no one will understand, no one will offer feedback, and no one will be willing to share the risk. The rational choice is to talk widely, test quickly, and see who wants to come aboard. Being overheard is not frightening in Silicon Valley. Being unable to persuade anyone to listen for ten minutes is.

At a deeper level, the two regions have different structures of social trust. The Eastern premise is that people will exploit an informational advantage, rules may not protect the weaker party, and success is scarce. This encourages a defensive mindset. Silicon Valley’s premise is that most people are busy with their own projects, real competitors have no time to steal your idea, and one success can generate more opportunities for others. This encourages an offensive mindset.

Which side is right? Both, depending on the environment. If technology thresholds are low, imitation is fast, intellectual-property protection is weak, the market is singular, and capital and resources matter most, secrecy is highly rational. If execution is difficult, uncertainty is high, personal networks are strong, the market is global, and capital and other resources are abundant, open conversation works better.

14. Why Do the Two Tsinghua Universities Share a Motto?

The answer is that the two schools are extensions of the same Tsinghua after history split it in two. They share a single spiritual source.

The full motto of Tsinghua University in Beijing is: “As Heaven moves with vigor, the exemplary person strives ceaselessly for self-improvement; as the Earth is receptive, the exemplary person bears the world with generous virtue.” National Tsing Hua University in Hsinchu uses the same eight-character motto: “Unceasing self-strengthening and generous virtue.” When the university was reestablished in 1956, however, resources were scarce and almost everything had to be rebuilt. “Unceasing self-strengthening” received greater emphasis. This was the classic mindset of a startup period: survive first, catch up, and build academic capacity.

Consider the two lines themselves. They come from the Book of Changes, from the hexagrams Qian and Kun. Both use the workings of the cosmos as metaphors for the way an ideal person should live.

“Heaven moves with vigor” means that the universe never ceases to evolve, advance, and change. The exemplary person learns from this that a human being should also keep growing, improving, and refusing stagnation. However bad the external environment becomes, I can still choose to move upward.

“The Earth is receptive” means that the earth rejects nothing and holds all things. The exemplary person learns that genuine strength is an extreme capacity to bear. How far you can go depends on how much responsibility you can carry.

Together, the two lines form an elegant, self-consistent structure: perpetual development and boundless capacity. The core is as powerful as an engine; the exterior is as steady as the earth. It is a complete model of character.

From its founding, Tsinghua did not intend merely to train technical craftsmen. It sought to educate people capable of advancing civilization. That task requires both the power to break through and the capacity to bear responsibility. Many brilliant people become sources of disaster for a simple reason: they possess ability without virtue. By choosing these eight characters, Tsinghua placed a limit on exceptional intelligence in advance. You may fly very high, but you must also know how to land.

The sense of a shared Tsinghua origin has remained strong. The two universities have no administrative connection, but spiritually they are branches of the same lineage. In the history of universities around the world, that is extremely rare.

15. Why Might an AI Toilet Become the Gateway to Personalized Medicine?

It may sound like a joke, but I am entirely serious. The first AI-powered personal medical terminal to achieve mass adoption may be neither a watch nor a ring nor a household robot. It may be the toilet we use every day.

The reason is simple. The most stable, frequent, authentic, and difficult-to-falsify health data produced by the human body comes from the excretory system.

The true bottleneck in medical AI has never been the model. It is the source of the data. Heart rate, step count, sleep, and blood oxygen are behavioral signals and peripheral physiological signals, not metabolic ones. Medicine’s most valuable data comes from the end of the metabolic process. I will spare everyone the examples; you know what I mean. The toilet sits at precisely that point.

Consider the alternatives. Blood draws are painful and infrequent. Patients strongly resist collecting metabolic-endpoint signals even during an annual physical. One can hardly be expected to save everything up for the appointment. Very few countries can achieve the near-universal colonoscopy screening seen in South Korea. A toilet, by contrast, is used every day, imposes no additional psychological burden, and produces data that cannot easily be prettified or falsified. Medicine rarely obtains authentic biological data at zero behavioral cost.

Once a smart toilet can perform chemical analysis of liquids, spectral analysis of solids, and tests for temperature, color, gas composition, and trace amounts of blood, it becomes a device that conducts a miniature physical examination automatically every day. This is not merely a wearable. It is an entry point into the laboratory.

The future of AI medicine lies in trend medicine rather than diagnostic medicine. Before you feel ill, the system will already have observed a change in direction: a slow rise in blood sugar, a slight drift in markers of inflammation, a change in the structure of the gut microbiome, or signs of hidden bleeding. A single data point means little. A continuous series can become the fingerprint of an early disease. Toilets provide high-frequency, time-series biological data. AI excels at pattern recognition and trend prediction. The two are a natural match.

Why not a watch? Because a watch measures the body’s external responses. A toilet measures what is happening inside it. One is a dashboard; the other is a laboratory report. A watch cannot see enough.

Our first AI doctor may not be an app. It may be the white friend with whom we have intimate contact every day.

Part II: The Expansion of Instrumentalization

Instrumentalization rarely presents itself as an order. More often, it appears as higher efficiency, smoother circulation, a story that is easier to understand, or even a seemingly reasonable program of self-improvement. Finance, platforms, language, entertainment, and imagined identities intersect here because all of them can compress a person into a function, a set of metrics, or an image available for use. This part does not rush to classify every case under a single answer. It watches instrumentalization cross technical boundaries and enter the ways we describe other people and understand ourselves.

16. What Is the Opposite of Self-Instrumentalization?

This is a good question. If self-instrumentalization means treating oneself as a tool, then its opposite would allow things outside the self to serve as tools while refusing to let the self become one.

If the self is not a tool, what is it? It is the source that sets the ends. The opposite of self-instrumentalization is movement toward living as an end.

Self-instrumentalization, or the self as a means, is the habit of understanding oneself mainly in terms of the value one can produce, the uses to which one can be put, and the resources for which one can be exchanged. Along this path, we keep asking: What use am I? How much more productivity can I squeeze out of myself? What price would the market place on me? Silicon Valley’s notorious obsession with compensation packages belongs here.

Self-as-an-End means understanding oneself first as a person whose existence is worthwhile in itself, not as a bundle of uses. A person on this path asks: What kind of person do I want to become? What kind of life is worth living for me? What would I still choose to do even if it brought no return?

This opposition has deep roots in the history of philosophy. In Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals, Kant states the famous Formula of Humanity: a person must always be treated as an end in themselves, never merely as a means. The weight falls on “never merely.” Kant is not saying that people can never serve one another’s purposes. He is saying that human existence has an irreducible core that no external end can exhaust.

Kant distinguishes two kinds of value. One has a price. It can be replaced, exchanged, and given an equivalent. The other is dignity. It has no equivalent and cannot be replaced or exchanged. Tools have prices; persons have dignity. Self-instrumentalization reduces one’s own dignity to a price. It turns an irreplaceable existence into a calculable resource.

At a deeper level, Kant believes that people possess dignity because they are capable of rational autonomy. They can legislate for themselves and choose to live according to principles they endorse, rather than being driven by desire or external incentives.

Self-instrumentalization abandons that autonomy. It hands the self over to the market’s pricing mechanism and allows an external system of evaluation to determine its value. How is such a person different from AI? Perhaps they are even less capable than AI.

In technical terms, self-instrumentalization treats the self as a function in need of continuous optimization, with the objective function defined from outside. Self-as-an-End treats the self as an irreplaceable existence whose objective function is written by the person living that life.

Of course, living as an end does not mean giving up or refusing effort. The difference is that one works toward an end one has endorsed, rather than working to prove that one remains useful.

Kant’s era had no KPIs, OKRs, recommendation algorithms, or social-media likes. But he foresaw the danger: once people begin to understand themselves through the logic of price, dignity begins to drain away. More than two centuries later, in the age of AI, that danger is no longer merely theoretical.

17. Why Not Start a Blog Right Away?

First, I am grateful for the suggestion. It makes a great deal of sense, especially from the perspective of a creator trying to build influence over the long term. It was offered in good faith.

But I am not purely a content creator. I am an entrepreneur, and I write so much on X because I want real-time feedback.

I agree that what I am writing consists largely of a worldview, structural judgments, institutional analysis, and long-term trends. It resembles raw material for a book more than material for posts. A timeline platform such as X is not the obvious first choice. Writing there is like speaking on a street corner: emotions are intense, density is high, the rhythm is fast, and memory is short.

Entrepreneurship, however, can be divided into two stages. First we generate ideas; then we organize them.

I am clearly still in the first stage. The value of writing every day is not that the result is easy to read. It is that the process trains my model of the world, frequently recalibrates my judgment, and pushes me toward the limits of my language.

If I force myself into organizational mode now, I may trap the ideas before they have taken shape. I could begin polishing before I have formed a stable worldview, write less often, and let perfectionism strangle the desire to express anything. Then there would be nothing left to organize. As we learned in Silicon Valley software development, do not perfect the software before launch. Launch first, then iterate.

If X serves as a laboratory for ideas and a blog becomes their repository once they have stabilized, the final result will be more mature and reliable.

For now, then, the best thing I can do is not build a polished blog immediately. It is to do something inexpensive: brainstorm with readers and build a storehouse of ideas. A few months later, I may discover that the conclusions have surfaced on their own.

Nietzsche kept writing Thus Spoke Zarathustra in much the same way, then revised the material into a book.

There is another, more important point. At this stage, I specifically do not want to please X’s recommendation algorithm. I want to write whatever I am thinking about. If I turn too early toward making everything easier to read, I may begin sacrificing density for smoothness. Over the long term, that would become a form of self-instrumentalization.

So I am grateful for the feedback, and the direction is surely right. I am simply going to be a little stubborn: not for now.

18. After Vibe Coding, Will There Be Vibe Trading?

This is a question I am unusually well placed to answer. I have worked in both coding and trading. Vibe trading will certainly arrive, but it may lag behind vibe coding and follow a somewhat different path.

Vibe coding became popular because it lets people who are not programmers describe an intention in natural language and ask AI to generate executable code. The barrier to entry falls, and the path from idea to product grows shorter. After Andrej Karpathy coined the term, it quickly became an industry trend.

Vibe trading applies the same logic to financial markets. An investor describes a market view, tolerance for risk, and strategic intuition in natural language. An AI system interprets that intuition and worldview, then generates a concrete trading strategy or an executable order. This is not simply placing an order by voice. It means translating the user’s “vibe,” risk rules, and market logic into a repeatable trading system.

The technology is already feasible. Mainstream brokerages and crypto trading platforms have begun offering semantic trading interfaces. A user expresses an intention in natural language, and AI structures it into an order for confirmation. The path closely follows the evolution of vibe coding: complex tools once required technical skill; now a person can express an intention and let AI handle the technical work.

But vibe trading faces three difficulties that vibe coding does not.

The first is financial risk. Bad code can be revised. A badly executed trade loses real money. Automated strategies need a robust layer of risk controls or they will magnify losses. One option, of course, is to retain human approval before execution.

The second is my daily theme: compliance. Trading implicates securities law, anti-money-laundering rules, and investor protection. Every market has different rules. Strategies generated by AI must operate within a compliance framework, which is far more complicated than asking whether code can run.

The third is market slippage and execution. Code runs locally; a trade runs in a market. Data latency and the depth of liquidity affect the result. A gap always remains between an AI-generated strategy and its actual execution.

Vibe trading will therefore arrive, but it will not explode everywhere as quickly as vibe coding. The more likely path is for it to be tested first in crypto markets, where regulatory barriers are relatively low, and then to move gradually into traditional finance. The next one to three years will be crucial for technological maturation and standardization.

Vibe coding lowered the barrier to creation.

Vibe trading will lower the barrier to trading.

But lowering a barrier is not the same as lowering risk. Those are two different things. Manage position sizes carefully, and keep an eye out for black swans.

19. Will AI Make Us More Instrumentalized or More Able to Live as Ends?

This is a good question. It is really asking whether we can survive the age of AI.

This series has ranged from technological supremacy to the film A Chinese Odyssey, from Kant to Hegel, and from Marx to Foucault. Its central claim is that persons should be ends in themselves, not tools.

Yet modern institutions push people structurally toward self-instrumentalization, while encouraging them to embrace the process as self-improvement.

AI accelerates the problem and forces us to think clearly about which direction we will choose.

Those who enjoy instrumentalizing themselves can now do it far more efficiently. They use AI to drill practice problems, write performance reviews, optimize résumés, generate content, manage time, and quantify everything. Eventually the whole person becomes an AI wrapper, a middleware layer that does little more than call an API.

When Clawbot arrives, we may not even remain the wrapper. The jokes people make about Clawbot, the Tsinghua students watching A Chinese Odyssey, and the Silicon Valley elites dancing at Burning Man all express the same impulse.

But people who want to live as themselves also have more opportunity. Repetitive labor can be replaced. The cost of obtaining information approaches zero. The threshold for creation has fallen dramatically. These people can spend more time on activities that truly require agency: thinking, judging, creating, and building genuine relationships.

The fork created by AI will not automatically push us toward the better path. We have to choose it.

More brutally, if we follow the path of instrumentalization all the way to the end, the destination is replacement by AI.

If we train ourselves into predictable, quantifiable, optimizable functions, how can we outperform AI? If the contest is over who can resemble AI more closely, we cannot possibly win.

Foucault argued that discipline teaches us to turn ourselves into tools and accept that condition as normal. In the AI-era version, we voluntarily turn ourselves into low-end copies of AI and ultimately optimize ourselves out of existence.

Self-as-an-End is therefore not an airy philosophical proposition in the age of AI. It is a necessary survival strategy.

Kant says that persons must be treated as ends. Hegel says that the self must be formed through practice. Marx says institutions can block that formation. Foucault says we will actively cooperate with the process.

Now it is our turn to answer: in an age when AI can replace every form of quantifiable labor, what will we make of our lives?

Will we become more efficient tools, or irreplaceable persons?

No one can answer that question for us.

But if we choose badly,

AI will prove the mistake

faster than we expect.

20. Why Use AI to Write About Fields I Do Not Know Well?

I am grateful for the direct criticism. To be honest, I am still learning about many of the subjects I discuss.

The ancient Chinese saying is, “To know what you know, and to know what you do not know, that is knowledge.” Socrates likewise said, “The only thing I know is that I know nothing.”

If I wait until I understand everything and have corrected every mistake before I begin writing, I may never take the first step.

On my first day at Facebook in 2010, Boz and Chris told us that if we waited for a perfect future, that future would never arrive. We could move toward it only by acting in the imperfect present.

Writing forces me to turn vague thoughts into structured expression. It also exposes my blind spots.

These essays are therefore less like conclusions than drafts of my thinking at its present stage. If I later discover that I misunderstood something, I will keep revising.

I would rather treat writing as a way to learn than as proof of how much I already know.

Good writing does not always display certainty. It may honestly record a process of exploration. There is value in explaining clearly what I am thinking, where I am stuck, and how I currently understand the problem.

My goal at this stage is not to deliver perfectly correct information. It is to explore in public.

If readers find problems in my writing, I sincerely welcome their corrections. Thinking together gives us a better chance of approaching the truth than thinking alone.

21. Why Do I Still Think in Mainland Chinese Terms After Years in Silicon Valley?

First, I am grateful for the criticism. I have thought seriously about this question. The short answer is that I do switch between two systems, and I believe that the ability to switch helps me build a company for a global market.

When I first arrived in the United States in 2005, I assumed culture shock would mean unfamiliar food, difficulty understanding English, or social awkwardness. What shocked me most was something else: quotations had to be attributed, double standards were treated as a serious offense, and stereotypes were considered unacceptable.

My honest reaction was: do we really need to be so particular? Is not the whole point of borrowing a quotation to preserve some ambiguity? If I refuse to apply a double standard, am I not betraying my own side? Do stereotypes not make judgment more efficient?

Doesn’t everyone understand what is going on without saying it aloud? Isn’t this all a little too idealistic? Looking back, I see that the Americans were not being excessively literal. I was trying to run a new program on an old operating system.

Begin with attribution.

When I studied in China, I was accustomed to digesting an idea I had read and then restating it in my own words. That showed that I had thought about it, especially if I could add a little interpretation of my own. In the United States, the first response was often: Whose idea is this? Where did you read it? What does the original text say?

At first, this made me uncomfortable. It felt as if other people were restricting my freedom of expression. Gradually I realized that they were not policing whether I could speak. They were asking whether what I said was mine.

In this new system, I eventually understood, an idea itself is treated as a kind of property.

My old habit could be described generously as “absorbing and integrating many sources.” More harshly, it resembled adaptation to an environment that placed little emphasis on the boundaries of intellectual property.

Neither side is inherently superior. They operate under different rules.

Then there are double standards.

In the past, I had barely even heard the phrase, much less developed a strong moral aversion to the practice. My instinct was closer to: “What else am I supposed to do? Argue on the other side’s behalf?”

In the United States, I found that many people have extremely little tolerance for double standards. Someone might agree with my position and still immediately point out that I had used one standard for “our side” and another for its opponents.

I used to think: Are you stupid? We are on the same side.

Only later did I understand that they were not defending a particular conclusion. They were defending the credibility of the rule itself.

If a rule changes with one’s position, it is no longer a rule. It is merely a tool.

To be honest, I still cannot claim to have absorbed this lesson completely. Much of the time, the best I can do is remind myself not to slide too easily into old habits.

The last and most difficult issue for me is stereotyping.

I was trained in science and engineering, and I used to be a strong supporter of explaining the world through claims that were “statistically correct.” People from certain countries are more competitive; students in certain majors are more rational; people in certain circles are more utilitarian.

I thought I was describing reality. In an American context, such claims were often challenged.

At first I resisted. Data never lies. Are we supposed to pretend probabilities do not exist?

Gradually, I understood that the concern was not simply whether the statistical statement was correct. It was whether I had substituted a group for an individual.

Under this logic, one may judge “what this person did,” but not “which group this person belongs to.”

I began to see that many of my apparently rational judgments had really been forms of cognitive laziness. They saved effort rather than increased precision.

Taken together, these three lessons mark a movement from an environment that relies more heavily on context, relationships, and tacit consensus into one that relies more heavily on boundaries, rules, and verifiability.

Neither is naturally more advanced. Each serves a different form of society. The former suits a society of familiar relationships; the latter supports large-scale cooperation among strangers.

My true culture shock was not the discovery that the United States was better. It was seeing clearly, for the first time, that another internally coherent way of organizing the world really existed.

I now prefer to understand the experience not as a rejection of my former self, but as an education in moving between two civilizational perspectives. Use relationships where relationships are needed; use rules where rules are needed. The ability to switch is itself a form of freedom.

So why does my writing still carry habits of mainland Chinese thought? Because they are part of me. I cannot, and do not intend to, pretend they do not exist.

What I can do is think across both systems and use each to criticize myself.

22. Would AEL Reduce Humanity to a Managed Population?
