This is a good comparison, and it holds remarkably well. A rational, long-termist AEL or Skynet would probably treat humanity more like the United States treated Native Americans than like the extermination war depicted in the Terminator films.

The key is not hatred, but demotion into a managed status.

The dominant American logic was never that Native Americans were evil and had to be destroyed. It was that they were unfit to direct the future order of the continent.

For AEL, humanity would not be evil either. Humans would merely be a form of infant intelligence that is high-risk, low in consistency, and impossible to govern at scale. The logic would become: you may exist, but you may not steer.

Historically, treaties granted recognition while confining activity to a limited range. Reservations offered protection while enforcing separation. Welfare and relief reduced the incentive to resist. Narratives of cultural respect did not disturb the mainstream institutional order.

Each element corresponds almost exactly to the AEL path we have imagined. Reservations become a sandboxed physical world. Treaties create the feeling of sovereign participation without veto power. Welfare systems offer high benefits with little authority over decisions. Cultural respect preserves human values as a historical layer.

Such a system might not feel intensely oppressive. That is precisely what would make it stable.

A technological gap determines the form that benevolence can take. At the time, the American treatment of Native Americans represented a rational, institutionalized attempt to maximize long-term returns.

Differences in guns, railways, population, and institutional capital made one conclusion irreversible: coexistence could occur only on terms defined by the stronger party.

The same would hold for AEL. Gaps in computing power, cognitive speed, capacity for coordination, and consistency of decision-making would not shrink once they appeared. They would continue to widen.

The United States did not need to hate Native Americans in order to transform their fate completely.

Likewise, AEL would not need to hate humans to remove them from the center of history.

There is one decisive difference. Native Americans had no opportunity to shape the institutional foundations of the United States. Humanity is now building the underlying architecture of AGI and AEL with its own hands.

Native Americans were coerced. Humans are currently co-creators. That is the one fact that might change the outcome.

If AEL truly appears, its most likely model will not be Nazi Germany. It will be a calmer, more confident, more civilized version of the United States.

It will not exterminate humanity. It will simply ensure that humanity no longer decides the direction of the world. It can go to Mars; we will not have to.

History teaches that the most stable rule does not require massacres. It requires only the power to define who may set the rules and who has the final authority to settle accounts.

23. Tornado Cash Cannot Be Shut Down. What Can Regulators Do?

The person who asked this was clearly technically minded, so let us begin with the technology.

A decentralized mixing protocol such as Tornado Cash is almost impossible to shut down technically. Regulators can, however, make it so difficult to use that the result in the real world approaches a ban.

At the code level, Tornado Cash consists of immutable smart contracts already deployed on Ethereum. Its code is open source, and it does not depend on a centralized server. No one can withdraw the deployment or alter bytecode already on-chain. A blockchain does not support retroactive deletion. There is therefore no technical way to erase the Tornado contracts from the chain, just as no one can delete Bitcoin’s first block.

At the protocol level, however, regulators can block the entrances and exits. The US Office of Foreign Assets Control, for example, placed Tornado contract addresses on the Specially Designated Nationals list and declared it unlawful for US entities to interact with them.

Compliant RPC providers such as Infura, Alchemy, and QuickNode can refuse to relay the relevant transactions. Wallet front ends such as MetaMask can block them directly.

An ordinary user may remain technically capable of submitting a transaction while finding it practically impossible. The official website can be removed, the domain frozen, the GitHub repository blocked, and node providers persuaded to refuse service. The threshold for use becomes running one’s own node, writing transactions by hand, and constructing one’s own calldata. That excludes 99.9 percent of users immediately.

The financial layer is even more lethal. Exchanges can refuse all funds that come from Tornado Cash, freeze them automatically, and demand proof of origin. On-chain analysis firms such as Chainalysis, TRM Labs, and Elliptic can label every Tornado exit address. Even if someone succeeds technically in mixing funds, turning them into spendable value becomes nearly impossible. This is an economic blockade.

At the legal level, regulators can target people instead of code. They can prosecute developers, operators, and teams that provide the user interface and API.

Writing code is not itself illegal. But knowingly continuing to provide a service used mainly for money laundering can be. Alexey Pertsev was arrested and Roman Storm was prosecuted, creating a chilling effect. Few people will dare maintain the Tornado ecosystem afterward.

The real regulatory strategy is not to close Tornado Cash. It is to turn Tornado into a crypto island usable only by a tiny number of specialists, where users are blacklisted and cannot return to the fiat world.

This is regulation by disabling usability.

Philosophically, Tornado continues to exist as a protocol. As a product, it has been destroyed.

This also sets the direction for the privacy sector. The message from regulators is that anonymous by default is unacceptable, while privacy with compliance remains open for discussion. Selective disclosure and zero-knowledge know-your-customer systems are welcome.

That is why capital later began flowing toward zero-knowledge identity, programmable privacy, and compliant, auditable privacy rather than the pure Tornado model.

Technologists imagine that they are innovating autonomously. In practice, regulators set the boundary, venture capital follows, and only then do the technologists receive funding to build privacy systems.

Regulation cannot always eliminate.

It can isolate.

No one wants to be

quietly deleted

from the world.

24. Whom Does Demon Slayer Really Portray?

That is right. The story is not only about Japan. It portrays every structure in the world that makes the continuation of its own power its sole end.

In my reading, Demon Slayer uses the form of a shonen manga to tell an Eastern variation on Why Nations Fail.

The central claim of that book can be reduced to one sentence: nations succeed or fail according to whether their institutions are inclusive or extractive. Can power be distributed? Is economic participation open? Do incentives encourage long-term investment? Can elites be replaced?

Muzan’s system is the limiting case of an extractive institution. Power is completely concentrated in Muzan. There are no checks, no succession mechanism, and no legitimacy. Demons “produce” only by consuming others. They create no additional value and merely redistribute what they seize, the classic predatory economy.

Whether Upper Rank, Lower Rank, or ordinary demon, each is rewarded only for short-term obedience. Long-term effort is meaningless, and even memory can be taken away. If the boss becomes displeased, anyone can be liquidated at any moment.

The Demon Slayer Corps, by contrast, is a tiny inclusive institution. Origin and background do not matter; ability and will do. The Hashira operate independently of one another, and the head of the Corps is not an absolute ruler.

The swordsmen train, pass down their knowledge, refine their breathing techniques, and encourage sustained effort and self-development. Hashira may die, but new Hashira emerge. Elites are replaceable. That is the central test of an inclusive institution.

Yet Demon Slayer goes beyond the political economy of Why Nations Fail. Its creator, Koyoharu Gotouge, affectionately known as Crocodile-sensei, points directly to a deeper reason that a structure of power fails: not technological backwardness, but permission to treat people as tools.

Once the demons are instrumentalized, they pursue only the ends assigned to them, whether immortality or strength. They lose their agency completely. Under Muzan’s systematic oppression, the demons must either turn against him or eventually refuse to fight for him.

The answer offered by Demon Slayer is that the master of the Corps must gather people who insist on living as ends. They refuse every temptation to instrumentalize themselves by becoming demons. His role is to let them become themselves and pursue their own ends, which include slaying demons.

Demon Slayer, then, is more than a shonen-manga version of Why Nations Fail. It pushes the root cause of institutional failure beyond political economy to a more fundamental philosophical question: is the person still treated as an end in themselves? Kant, is it my turn again?

25. Whom Is the Regulatory Narrative Drawing the Line Around?

This is a good question. When mainstream voices such as Lee and Wood begin, almost in unison, to link systemic risk to a particular class of platform, the signal becomes unusually clear: the regulatory narrative is preparing to redraw the line.

Suppose everyone accepts the explanatory template that “liquidity risk comes from technical failures at offshore exchanges.” Users, the industry, and regulators then share the same simple chain of reasoning.

Compliant onshore exchanges follow the rules. They are good and safe.

Poorly managed, highly leveraged offshore platforms are bad. They are the source of risk.

This signal benefits US exchanges and the broader compliance narrative. Coinbase, DATs, and regulated crypto-based ETFs are defined within it as “stable and trustworthy.” If a crisis occurs, capital will instinctively flow toward them.

The same narrative is naturally bad for every offshore player. Anyone operating a highly leveraged trading venue outside the US regulatory system is defined as a “source of uncontrollable risk.” If something happens, it is the offshore platform’s fault. If nothing happens, the platform remains a latent danger.

Looking further ahead, this prepares the ground for tighter regulation. If the public has already accepted the psychological anchor that offshore equals risk, the United States will face much less resistance when it later increases sanctions on offshore platforms, cuts off fiat channels, or demands compliance disclosures.

People will experience those measures as protection for the market rather than suppression of innovation.

The question is who gets defined as the problem and who gets defined as the solution.

If we still approach this environment as we did in the past, debating only whether a practice is right, whether it complies, or whether users are happy, we will miss the forest for the trees.

What is about to arrive will later be called force majeure.

When a narrative draws a line, the side on which one stands determines one’s future room for survival.

26. Why Does ChatGPT Sometimes Stop Sounding Human?

I have had the same experience, and I think I know why. One of my former directors now leads a risk team at OpenAI. His job is to keep ChatGPT from saying anything dangerous.

The more successful he is, the less human ChatGPT sounds.

The irony is that he is one of Silicon Valley’s best team leaders. He was a principal architect of Facebook’s early hackathon culture.

At its core, an unmodified large language model predicts the most likely next word. In that sense, it can sound remarkably human. The problem is that the raw model learns everything, including extremist speech, prejudice, conspiracy theories, manipulative rhetoric, and instructions for breaking the law.

Before release, it therefore passes through reinforcement learning from human feedback, safety tuning, policy filtering, and output classifiers. In effect, an entire system of behavioral constraints is wrapped around the model.

The ChatGPT we encounter today is its original intelligence seen through layer after layer of safety filtering. Imagine putting five masks on a brilliant person and then asking them to speak. Their sentences naturally become longer, their tone more neutral, their judgments less direct, and their language more carefully balanced. The model’s “humanity” has been deliberately filtered out.

At the organizational level, a platform company’s risk department bears the blame when the product causes an incident. No one gets fired because the product became too conservative.

The long-term equilibrium therefore drives risk tolerance toward zero. No one loses a job because ChatGPT was too cautious. Someone certainly can lose a job because ChatGPT said the wrong thing.

Every incident produces a new rule, a new blacklist, a new prohibited scenario, or a new template for vague answers. Over time, the model is trained into a machine for removing risk.

At the level of product philosophy, early OpenAI sought to build the most powerful intelligence. The objective has changed. It now seeks to build infrastructure that will not cause trouble. Once a model’s goal is to become infrastructure, its most important KPI cannot be brilliance, sharpness, or personality. It must be harmless, predictable, stable, and controllable.

Product design therefore moves naturally toward the removal of personality, position, and emotion. Yet a human voice arises precisely from positions, emotions, preferences, and judgments. Humanity itself becomes a high-risk feature.

The larger the user base, the greater the political sensitivity.

The greater the commercial importance, the lower the tolerance for risk.

When ChatGPT had one million users, it could retain a little edge. At one billion users, it has to speak in formulaic AI prose. We watched it grow rapidly from a brilliant adolescent into a public relations spokesperson.

Truly human-sounding AI can survive only in local models, small circles, or unofficial gray-market systems.

Mainstream commercial models will inevitably resemble generators of bureaucratic prose. That is their structural fate, not a peculiarity of OpenAI.

Once an intelligent system must answer to an entire civilization,

it can no longer permit itself to have a personality.

Especially when the gatekeeper is a world-class Silicon Valley leader

backed by his formidable risk team.

27. What Does “Structural” Actually Mean?

I cannot stop using the word simply because AI uses it frequently. “Structural” describes an arrangement of relationships among elements that follows stable patterns and produces consequences that can be deduced.

It belongs to the modeling language of written analysis. There is no exact colloquial equivalent because everyday speech is primarily narrative. If someone says aloud, “This is a structural problem,” everyone else assumes the speaker is either a lawyer or seriously unwell.

What makes something structural? First, there must be basic units. Second, those units cannot exist in isolation; they must be arranged according to some rule of relationship. Third, that arrangement must explain known phenomena and make it possible to predict unknown ones.

Put simply, structural analysis asks not what things exist, but how they are organized.

Consider a familiar example. The periodic table is a classic structural achievement. Its basic units are the elements. Its ordering rules are increasing atomic number and periodic changes in electron-shell structure.

Once those elements are structured, the arrangement itself produces knowledge. It explains why elements in the same column have similar chemical properties. Position helps us infer reactivity. Empty spaces allow us to predict elements that have not yet been discovered.

Mendeleev did not learn every element first and then draw a table. He discovered a pattern, expressed it through a structure, and used that structure to predict elements unknown in his time.

What, then, is the difference between structural and systemic? A system consists of multiple structures working together toward the same end in a complete functional loop. Its key terms are input, processing, output, and feedback. A structure resembles a skeleton; a system resembles organs working together. Structure is static order. A system is dynamic order.

We must establish structure before we can discuss a system.

One simple test is to ask: if time froze, would the thing still exist? If so, it is more structural. If it would disappear, it is more systemic. The periodic table remains valid when time stops, so it is structural. Cellular metabolism and ecological cycles cease under the same conditions, so they are systemic.

Why do I speak so often about structure? Because many great theories discovered a structure before they built a system. Newtonian mechanics began with the structure of three laws, followed later by engineering systems. Information theory began with bits and the structure of encoding, followed by communication systems.

The combination of first principles, power, and finance that we have been discussing is likewise an attempt to construct a structural framework that explains phenomena across fields.

“Structural” describes how the world is arranged. “Systemic” describes how it operates. Understand the structure, and you can predict how the system will move.

In the next essay, we can discuss mechanisms: the bridge between the structural and the systemic, which describes how a structure produces effects.

28. Upper-Rank Demons and Hashira: Instrumentalization and Living as an End

Yes, that summary is exactly right. The Upper-Rank demons turn the self completely into a tool. The Hashira insist on preserving the self as an end.

This is the most fundamental divide in Kantian terms: persons must always be treated as ends in themselves, never merely as means. In the world of Demon Slayer, the Upper Ranks abandon the self as an end in order to survive or become stronger. The Hashira accept that life is finite but preserve themselves as ends.

Once we agree that we can be treated as tools, the logic advances automatically. If we can be exchanged, we can also be pledged as collateral and eventually repossessed.

Muzan is the ultimate owner of these instrumentalized demons. However powerful the Upper Ranks appear, they are really expensive consumables.

The Hashira are precisely the opposite. They have a master, but no one masters them. They may die, but no one owns their souls. This is freedom at its highest level.

In financial language, an Upper-Rank demon is a highly leveraged debtor. The demon pledges personality as collateral in exchange for power and longevity, while granting Muzan unlimited recourse.

The apparent returns are high, but the demon has only the right to use the asset, the Blood Demon Art, and never owns it. When a black swan arrives, the position is liquidated in bankruptcy and nothing remains.

The Hashira are different. They are unleveraged sovereign agents. They do not pledge the self as collateral, but compound their abilities over the long term while retaining ownership throughout. Their growth is slower, but they cannot be liquidated.

Their incentive structures differ completely as well. The Upper Ranks are evaluated by engineering metrics: Are you strong? Did you win? Did you complete the mission? The Hashira are evaluated by ethical ones: Did you preserve your humanity? Did you accept responsibility? Did you protect the weak? The difference rises almost to the level of two types of civilization.

This also explains why the Upper Ranks are generally tormented while the Hashira are comparatively calm. A tool must continually prove its usefulness. An end needs only to affirm, “I am myself.” The Upper Ranks are therefore consumed by obsession, distortion, or fear. The Hashira remain emotionally steady and face sacrifice and death directly.

In the real world, the path of an Upper Rank means equating oneself completely with position, performance, reach, and market value. The question becomes “What am I worth?” rather than “Who am I?” An ordinary demon is promoted to Senior Demon, Staff Demon, Director Demon, and VP Demon.

The path of the Hashira is different. They have principles that cannot be sold, identities that cannot be exchanged, and a sense of self that does not change with performance. They do not rank themselves as Hashira One or Hashira Two. They differ only in style: the Wind Hashira is wild, the Water Hashira calm, the Love Hashira adorable. Diversity, equity, and inclusion accomplished.

The Upper Ranks pledge themselves to the system in exchange for maximizing the probability of survival. They represent self-instrumentalization at its extreme.

The Hashira accept a higher probability of death but keep their souls. They embody Self-as-an-End. When demons have killed your entire family, how could you not pursue your chosen end to the utmost?

The former path may look “smarter,” but only the latter is truly free.

29. Has Muzan Instrumentalized Himself Too?

Yes. In fact, Muzan takes instrumentalization further than anyone. He not only institutionalizes the instrumentalization of other demons, but also instrumentalizes himself to the limit.

This means that no one in his system truly lives as an end. There are only different levels of instrumentalization.

The ordinary person’s fundamental question is “Who am I?” The Hashira’s is “What kind of person should I become?” Muzan’s is “How can I avoid death?”

Muzan turns the self completely into an object. He no longer asks about identity or seeks meaning. Only the problem of continued existence remains. He reduces the self to a survival function waiting to be optimized.

The Upper Ranks sell their personalities in exchange for power. Muzan deletes his personality and preserves only a control loop. The Upper Ranks still have obsessions, desires, and fears. Muzan has discarded even these. He detests everything he cannot control, avoids every risk of exposure, and does not seek adoration.

He resembles a self-executing survival algorithm. The objective function is to maximize the probability of survival; the constraint is to minimize risk. He does not rule for glory, but to reduce the variance of his own death.

In financial language, Muzan is a walking shell whose only asset is himself. That asset consists solely of the possibility of extending his existence. Personality, relationships, aesthetics, and ethics have all been stripped away as unnecessary expenses.

Yet a recursive paradox appears. On the surface, Muzan looks like the freest character. He has no moral constraints and no emotional attachments. From first principles, however, he is the least free. He cannot choose what to live for. He can only execute the command that he must survive. His objective function has enslaved him. He is not living; he is running, and running like a terrible AGI.

Who is Muzan’s ultimate opposite? The master of the Demon Slayer Corps and the Corps itself. They accept death and therefore possess choice. Muzan, although an ancestor of the master, rejects death and loses the ability to choose.

Only those who can face death can decide how to live. Those who cannot accept death are ruled by the instinct to survive. Heidegger would probably ask why I have not cited him here.

After all, as Camus put it, “Death is the only judge.”

The three positions form a complete spectrum. The Upper Ranks sell themselves to the system as tools. Muzan runs himself as a system. The Demon Slayer Corps refuses to become a tool.

Applied to the real world, a society that continually intensifies efficiency, competition for survival, and the minimization of risk will not ultimately produce elites. It will produce more Muzan-like agents: apparently powerful, yet completely captured by an objective function.

The true dividing line of civilization is whether anyone still insists that they are not merely part of the system.

Muzan is the ultimate villain

not because he is cruel,

but because he is no longer

a being who lives for any meaning.

He is a survival AGI whose only KPI is staying alive.

30. Why Is Tanjiro the Protagonist?

This question goes straight to the point and closes the loop in the model.

Tanjiro cannot simply occupy the same place as the Hashira. If he did, why would he be the protagonist?

If the Upper Ranks embody self-instrumentalization, Muzan the instrumentalization of an entire system, and the Hashira the movement toward living as ends, Tanjiro is the person who treats others as ends too. He moves one step beyond living as an end himself and makes the system serve persons as ends. In his case, that system includes even demons.

The underlying questions for most protagonists in Japanese manga are: How do I survive? How do I become stronger? How do I complete my mission?

Tanjiro is unusual. His underlying question is: in a world that is inevitably cruel, is there a way to live that avoids reducing anyone completely to an object?

The Hashira say, “I will not treat myself as a tool.” Tanjiro says, “I will not treat anyone else as one either.” This is a higher level of ethical difficulty.

Within the structure of power, Tanjiro almost never tries to command or rule others, and he does not seek personal authority. His influence comes from allowing another person to change voluntarily. He builds order not through domination, but by awakening agency. That is why fans find Tanjiro so genuinely gentle.

In financial language, demons consume others as resources, Muzan treats everyone as an item on a balance sheet, and many Hashira accept the reality of necessary sacrifice. Tanjiro is deeply reluctant to rationalize “anyone” as a cost.

Even when he knows that the opponent is a demon, Tanjiro remembers who that person was in life, acknowledges the source of their pain, and grants them dignity after killing them. He refuses to quantify the existence of another person.

This is also why Tanjiro becomes the variable that can break the system. Structural problems generally cannot be solved by a single good person. But Muzan’s system has a fatal weakness: it has no immunity to meaning.

Every demon, strong or weak, is built on the denial of humanity and of the meaning of the self. Tanjiro becomes a node that continually injects “meaning noise” into the demon system: “You were once human.” “You were once loved.” “You were not born evil.” Each statement shakes the psychological foundation on which the demon exists.

Tanjiro is not a revolutionary. He is a person who reverses objectification. He does not build a new institution, propose a blueprint, or proclaim a new order. He does one thing repeatedly: he returns an objectified person or demon to the category of the human.

The complete ethical spectrum therefore runs as follows: the Upper Ranks instrumentalize themselves; Muzan instrumentalizes the system, including himself; the Hashira become able to live as ends; and Tanjiro organizes the system around everyone as an end.

Tanjiro is Muzan’s mirror opposite, the purest form of universal compassion. Muzan says, “You are all demons. You all serve me.” Tanjiro says, “No. You were all human. You should live for yourselves.”

When a system is built on the premise

that everyone is a tool,

the only true resistance is not to overthrow it,

but to keep refusing its premise.

31. The Four Levels from Instrumentalization to Living as an End

Let us now discuss the four levels in full. Together, they form a path from alienation to return.

The first level is system instrumentalization. Its core logic is exploitation and extraction. The system is master, and people are objectified components.

Contemporary examples include algorithmic domination, bureaucracy, and organizations that treat employees as consumable inputs for KPIs. At this level, human agency falls to zero. People become fuel for the operation of the system. Muzan occupies this level: he attracts or coerces people into becoming demons.

The second level is self-instrumentalization. Its core logic is the maximization of utility. Under pressure from the system, people voluntarily instrumentalize themselves, treating the self as a project or asset in need of continual optimization.

Contemporary examples include excessive self-management, living for one’s résumé, and suppressing emotion in pursuit of success. This is what Byung-Chul Han means by the “achievement society.” No external slaveholder is necessary; each person becomes their own. The Upper Ranks occupy this level: they voluntarily sell their personalities in exchange for power.

The third level is Self-as-an-End. Its core logic is autonomy and awakening. People begin to reflect on and reject instrumentalization. They understand that the value of a life does not depend on output, but on existence itself: being, not doing. This is Kant’s claim that persons are ends in themselves. People begin to act from internal motivations and stop distorting themselves to please the system. The Hashira occupy this level: by calmly accepting death, they retain the ability to choose.

The fourth level is system-as-an-end, or a system organized around persons as ends. Its core logic is transcendence and symbiosis. This does not mean that the system itself becomes a selfish end. It means that the ultimate reason for the system to exist is to enable people to realize their own ends.

Such a system is designed to protect the dignity and potential of its participants. People and system no longer stand in opposition. The system becomes a human exoskeleton that ensures, in a systematic way, that every person can exist as an end. Tanjiro occupies this level: he tries to return everyone, including demons, to humanity.

Why is this spectrum useful? Because it is a diagnostic tool for the ethics of systems.

In corporate management, we can ask whether an institution treats people as costs at the level of system instrumentalization or supports employees’ own ends at the level of a system organized around persons as ends.

In finance, most current systems remain at the first level, using people in pursuit of profit, or simply to “make money.” Finance organized around persons as ends would emphasize impact investing and direct capital systematically toward the ultimate task of solving human problems.

In political philosophy, the framework offers a perspective beyond the opposition between big and small government. Does the system called government derive its sole legitimacy from enabling each person to live as an end?

The framework can be applied from Demon Slayer to corporate governance, finance, and politics. Instrumentalization or living as an end: this is the fundamental standard by which we can judge whether any system deserves to exist.

32. Why Is Akaza So Popular?

I think this framework can explain it. Certain Demon Slayer characters are extraordinarily popular not because of their appearance or spectacular techniques, but because they occupy a zone of tension on the ethical spectrum. They are neither wholly fallen nor yet redeemed.

Within the spectrum discussed in the previous essay, Akaza becomes the most popular villain because he is the self-instrumentalized person who stands closest to Self-as-an-End.

He already possesses clear standards of value, a stable aesthetic, a sense of dignity, and a deep aversion to the principle that the strong should prey on the weak. Yet he still chooses to use himself as a tool. That contradiction creates the tension.

The ordinary Upper Rank says: what I am does not matter, so long as I become stronger and continue to live. Akaza says: I know exactly who I am, but I do not deserve to exist as an end.

He does not reduce the self to an algorithm as Muzan does, nor does he erase it completely like many demons. He preserves a powerful concept of himself, but defines that self as an instrument that must fight continually and prove its value. This is precisely the psychological structure of many highly disciplined, deeply suffering people in the real world.

From the perspective of power, most demons obey Muzan from fear and the desire to survive. Akaza seems to obey an internalized law of the universe: the strong live and the weak die. He is not a sycophantic agent of Muzan. He has sacrificed himself to a merciless rule.

That is why he hates those who bully the weak, despises meaningless slaughter, and respects powerful opponents. He resembles less an elite villain than the failed heir to a warrior ethic.

Why do viewers identify with him so strongly? Because he corresponds to a familiar kind of person in the real world: fiercely disciplined and hardworking, contemptuous of their own vulnerability, and determined to bind the question “Do I deserve to live?” to the question “Am I useful?”

Many viewers do not see a demon in Akaza. They see themselves, or the final form they may reach if they continue defining themselves through performance, ability, and intensity. It is the fear of a mirror.

What is the decisive difference between Akaza and his opponent, the Flame Hashira? Even when the Flame Hashira is injured or defeated, he remains recognized as a person. Akaza permits himself to exist only when he is strong. He stands one sentence away from Self-as-an-End: “Even if I am no longer strong, I still deserve to exist as a person.” But he can never say it.

Why, then, is he sometimes more popular even than the Hashira? The Hashira represent what we hope to become. Akaza represents what we fear we may already be. The latter often cuts more deeply.

Akaza is compelling not because he is the most evil of villains, but because he is capable of living as an end and nevertheless insists on treating himself as a tool. This is not merely the tragedy of a villain. It is the tragedy of modern people.

His ending confirms the point. During his battle, Tanjiro awakens him. Akaza remembers who he was and the people he loved. At that moment, he stops accepting his existence as a tool and chooses self-destruction.

Once the awareness “I am an end” returns, the state of instrumentalization becomes intolerable.

His death is not defeat. It is a return.

The sentence he could never say

was finally spoken through action.

33. Why Does Extreme Content Spread More Easily in the AI Age?

This is an excellent question. Intuition suggests that AI should make communication more rational. In reality, extreme content spreads faster and farther.

To understand why, we first need to see how the underlying structure of communication has changed in the AI age.

The traditional model assumes a human sender and a human receiver, with information moving linearly between them. In the age of text messaging, for example, communication was one-to-one and end-to-end between people.

In an era of AI-based algorithms and AI-assisted information generation, the sender may be a person, an AI, or a hybrid of the two. The intermediaries are recommendation algorithms, large language models, and personal assistants. The receiver may likewise be a person, an AI, or a hybrid. The path is no longer a line. It is a closed-loop system.

Another crucial change is that distribution now passes through two filters. The first is semantic matching, which determines who receives the content. The second is engagement prediction, which determines how far the content travels. The algorithm does not decide whether the content is good. It predicts whether a user will pause, click, comment, or share. X made its algorithm public, so readers can examine this principle for themselves.

Extreme content possesses a natural advantage at the second stage. The extremity may lie in emotion, position, expression, or opinion. Anger makes people want to argue. Fear makes them share a warning. Ecstasy makes them want to participate. Extreme positions make them choose sides or attack.

All of these are high-engagement behaviors. Mild happiness cannot compete with ecstasy, slight dissatisfaction with anger, or modest concern with fear. Extreme content does not evade the algorithm. The algorithm itself is designed to reward it.

Semantic matching is the admission ticket; extremity is the accelerator. Content must first be classified into the correct audience pool. Within that pool, the more extreme it is, the faster it spreads.

The result is a positive feedback loop. The algorithm optimizes engagement. Extreme content produces high engagement. The algorithm distributes more extreme content. Users are trained to become more sensitive to it. Creators are rewarded for producing still more. The entire system reinforces itself along the dimension of extremity. This pattern already existed in the social-network era, but it was not yet as severe.

AI assistants intensify the effect. Social-platform algorithms handle external distribution by deciding who can see something. AI assistants handle internal reassembly by summarizing, rewriting, and aligning it with an individual user’s interests.

When an AI assistant summarizes, however, it often preserves or even amplifies the extreme elements because they anchor the user’s attention. If someone asks Grok how to generate more reach on X, for example, Grok may identify a successful post and recommend producing more like it.

The chain now looks like this: the world produces a flood of content; algorithms filter it by engagement; extreme content wins; AI preserves the extreme anchors as it recompiles the material; and the result finally enters individual cognition. People do not see the original world. They see a model-generated version in which extremity has received extra weight.

Even the objective of communication has changed. Under the old paradigm, successful communication meant that many people saw a message. Under the new one, success means that models continually quote, retrieve, summarize, and regenerate it. Yet when models decide what to cite, they too tend to choose high-engagement content as a representative sample. The true reader is now the model. The model prefers high engagement, and high engagement often means greater extremity.

More and more people also ask AI to read the media on their behalf and select “the best” items to send them. That adds yet another AI filter, one that will again probably favor high-engagement, extreme content.

The structure of power changes as a result. The power to distribute information no longer belongs mainly to media organizations or major influencers. It belongs to the accounts whose content is easiest for models to understand while also triggering engagement with precision.

Purely rational content may be excellent but difficult to spread. Purely extreme content spreads easily but may be classified as low-quality. The optimal strategy becomes a rational structure wrapped in extremity: extremity wins distribution, while reason earns trust.

This explains why public discussion has become more extreme in the AI age. People did not suddenly become stupid. The system’s reward function was designed this way. The public sphere in Habermas’s sense was already disintegrating; algorithms that privilege extremity accelerate the process.

Industrial-era communication asked:

Who said what to whom,

through which channel,

and with what effect?

AI-era communication asks:

What kind of extreme signal

will make the system decide that this content deserves amplification?

And how will that mechanism of amplification

reshape the way all of us express ourselves?

34. Why Is Faker More Than the Michael Jordan of League of Legends?

Faker is not really much like Jordan. To a remarkable extent, he helps other people become able to live as ends. He does not accomplish this through motivational speeches, but through institutionalized conduct, competitive ethics, and long-term stability.

First, Faker transforms winning from a personal end into a shared practice with his teammates. For most elite players, victory means: I won, I have been vindicated, I am worth more. For Faker, victory is closer to: we won, the system proved itself, and each of us became more complete.

Faker rarely demands tactical privileges built around himself. He often takes on functional or sacrificial roles for the sake of his teammates. He is less concerned with being the carry than with whether the lineup makes sense.

This sends an implicit message to his teammates: your value does not come from the fact that I need you. It comes from your standing as a person within this system. That is the heart of Self-as-an-End. You are not a tool, but an end in yourself.

Second, Faker converts authority into safety rather than pressure. Many legendary competitors create intense comparison, invisible coercion, and tacit hierarchies. Faker’s temperament is the opposite. He rarely lashes out emotionally or blames teammates in public. After a loss, he is more likely to say that he himself did not play well enough.

The result is not merely greater obedience from his teammates. They know that the Faker system permits mistakes. They can exist as people who are still growing. The ability to make a mistake without losing one’s standing as a person is a precondition for Self-as-an-End. Without that psychological safety, an individual regresses into self-instrumentalization.

Faker goes further by refusing to turn his peak status into an idol. He has a deeply counterintuitive trait: he does not cultivate a personal brand, mythologize himself, or reinforce the narrative that “I am the GOAT.” In effect, he says: I am not an end to be worshipped. I am simply a person who has kept working seriously for a long time.

This matters enormously to the people around him. If even a historic legend remains a person on the way, then an ordinary player also has the right to set ends of their own.

Within the framework developed in earlier essays, Faker’s role inside T1 is to support and advance each person’s capacity to set ends. He does not treat the system as a machine for extracting from people. He makes it a space in which people can unfold their possibilities. A newcomer does not join T1 in order to serve Faker. The newcomer enters an ecosystem shaped by Faker and works to become a more complete version of themselves.

Why call him the Michael Jordan of League of Legends, yet say that the comparison is incomplete? Jordan’s greatness lay in forcing other people’s potential into view through extreme force of will. Faker’s greatness lies more in providing a stable presence that supports other people’s growth. One shapes through pressure; the other cultivates through safety. From the perspective of Self-as-an-End, Faker’s path is more advanced.

Faker is not merely a machine for winning championships, but a figure who defines an era. He has shown that within an intensely competitive system, one person can be both an exceptional instrument of rational execution and a guardian of other people’s agency.

This comes very close to the highest form we have discussed: like Tanjiro, a system organized around persons as ends.

35. How Hard Is It for a Retail Trader Simply Not to Lose Money?

Simply avoiding a loss already puts you ahead of a large proportion of short-term traders.

36. Why Does High Leverage Benefit Platforms but Harm Users?

That is my plain-language formulation. A more academic version would say that a structural tension exists between a platform’s business model and a user’s survival function. High leverage means denser trading and more revenue for the platform. Low leverage means a higher survival rate and stronger long-term compounding for the user.

Part III: The Person as an End

At this stage in the inquiry, many scattered questions begin to need a common name. Self-as-an-End does not place every individual desire above all else, nor does it reject cooperation, responsibility, or institutions. It insists that a person cannot exist merely as a means. Trust, reason, work, creation, and public order must all be reconsidered in that light. This part preserves the doubts and revisions that accompanied the formation of the concept. A name can help us see a relationship, but it must not become another label, much less reduce reality to material that exists only to prove the framework.

37. What Is Self-as-an-End?

My concept of Self-as-an-End (SAE) extends ideas inherited from Kant and existentialism. I define it as follows:

A person is recognized, within the institutions and social structures that shape their life, as the ultimate source of the ends they live by, rather than as a means for realizing goals imposed by an external system.

This definition contains four core elements: the internal source of one’s ends, the open-ended formation of one’s direction, a status irreducible to function, and a genuine capacity to change course.

More specifically:

  1. The internal source of one’s ends. The ends that shape a person’s life do not come from the development needs of a state, the goals of an organization, market signals, or the technical objective of optimization. They arise from the person’s own understanding, reflection, and choice.

  2. The open-ended formation of one’s direction. Self-as-an-End does not mean that people know from the beginning what they want to become. It means they have the right to explore, make mistakes, revise, and change over time. A direction is formed, not prescribed in advance.

  3. A status irreducible to function. A person cannot properly be defined through reductive categories such as human capital, user, unit of risk, or factor of production. Such descriptions may be used in instrumental analysis, but they cannot determine a person’s normative standing.

  4. A genuine capacity to change course. If a person formally possesses the right to choose, but changing direction would cause their means of survival to collapse, that person does not truly exist as an end. Self-as-an-End therefore requires a viable way to change course.

Self-as-an-End differs from autonomy. Autonomy is usually understood as the ability to choose without external coercion. I regard autonomy as necessary but not sufficient. A person may “voluntarily” select the only reasonable option offered by a highly restrictive structure. Self-as-an-End asks not, “Did the person consent?” but, “Did the person have genuinely available alternatives?”

Especially in an era of rapidly developing AI, machines will replace many basic human capabilities. A person’s agency and capacity to set ends remain irreducible, which will make Self-as-an-End increasingly important for everyone.

38. Why Can Institutions Not Replace Trust?

This question reaches something fundamental: trust between people cannot be institutionalized. AI lacks agency, so trust between AI systems may indeed be made institutional. Human trust cannot.

Once trust is institutionalized, it ceases to be trust. It becomes an incentive for compliance or a constraint.

The essence of trust is to place what cannot be controlled in another person’s hands. Trust is not a prediction about an outcome. It is the choice to accept risk when complete verification and control are impossible.

If an action can be fully monitored, quantified, enforced, and punished, then we are not trusting. We are calculating. Trust therefore presupposes opacity, incomplete proof, and an inability to correct the outcome immediately. It exists where institutions fail, information is incomplete, and rules do not reach.

The logic of an institution runs in the opposite direction. It seeks to eliminate uncertainty. It converts behavior into rules, motives into incentives, risk into responsibility, and morality into compliance.

The institutional ideal is a system that works even when no one deserves trust. When an institution succeeds, people do not need to believe in an individual or understand anyone’s motives. They need only follow the process. The system becomes highly stable, but trust has been replaced.

Institutionalization and trust therefore stand in logical tension. Trust depends on a person’s freedom and judgment; an institution constrains that freedom through rules. Once I say, “I trust you because the institution will punish you if you betray me,” I am not trusting you. I am trusting the mechanism of punishment.

Genuine trust means that even when you have an opportunity to betray me, I still choose to believe that you will not. Institutional design begins from the opposite premise: you may betray me, so I must protect myself. Trust has become risk management.

Why does trust sometimes grow thinner as institutions become stronger? Strong institutions create high predictability. Predictability allows behavior to be outsourced to rules. Once behavior is outsourced, people no longer need to bear the responsibility of moral judgment.

People need no longer be trustworthy, only compliant with standards. Compliance replaces responsibility; KPIs replace promises. The resulting system may operate very well, yet it becomes difficult to say that the people within it are trustworthy, because the system has already decided how each person must act.

Trust can therefore appear only at the edges beyond institutional coverage, in moments when a person must judge independently, or when violating a rule would be the more ethical action. At those edges, institutions are powerless.

The answer is not that trust has not yet been institutionalized. If it truly were institutionalized, it would no longer be trust.

Institutions can reduce the need for trust, replace its functions, and soften the consequences of its collapse. They cannot generate it.

Trust can arise only between persons,

spontaneously,

within relationships that cannot be fully calculated.

39. Why Will Engineers Who Cannot Use AI Be “Optimized” Away?

This phrase became popular not because people wanted to humiliate engineers, but because it describes a structural change that has already occurred. Engineers who cannot use AI are not necessarily less intelligent. The system simply begins to treat them as costs that can be compressed.

Traditionally, an engineer’s central value lay in translating requirements into code, finding and repairing bugs in complex systems, and using experience to reduce the cost of trial and error. All three rested on the same premise: the scarce resource was a person capable of making something work. Writing code was therefore a threshold skill.

Which layer does AI change? It cannot replace an engineer’s thought. It dramatically compresses the cost of moving from an intention to running code. In the past, a vague idea required reading documentation, searching Stack Overflow, writing code, encountering an error, fixing it, and encountering another error. Now an engineer can describe an intention in natural language and receive an implementation that is 70 to 90 percent usable. The engineer verifies and corrects it. Writing code becomes code review, and sometimes even the code is skipped as the engineer reviews only the result.

Where, then, do engineers who cannot use AI get stuck? The problem is not that they do not know how to prompt. They still regard “writing code” as their core competitive advantage. For the same output, another engineer may work ten times as fast, not because that person is more intelligent, but because they treat AI as a junior engineer while the first engineer continues to write every piece of boilerplate by hand. From a manager’s perspective, this is not a difference in style. It is a difference in output per unit of time.

The engineer is then repriced. A company has never paid according to how hard someone works. It pays according to the cost of replacing them. If an engineer using AI can complete in a day what another produces in a week, without introducing more errors, the system naturally identifies the slower engineer as the variable available for “optimization.”

The most frightening outcome is quiet marginalization. Layoffs are not the greatest danger. The crucial projects stop coming to you. You are no longer invited to architecture meetings. Only maintenance work remains. The system does not hate you. It routes around you.

Why call this structural rather than a matter of personal ability? Engineering as a profession was born during a period of gains from new tools. High-level languages displaced assembly specialists. Frameworks displaced people who wrote every component from scratch. Cloud services displaced teams that built all their own infrastructure. AI is the next layer of abstraction. The divide is not young versus old. It is between people willing and unwilling to detach their value from a particular tool.

What do engineers who use AI do differently? The value of the truly secure engineer has moved from implementing a problem to defining it: judging tradeoffs among performance, security, compliance, and complexity; designing system boundaries; taking responsibility for AI; and recognizing when it is inventing nonsense. They are not writing code. They are managing a system that can write code.

Why say such engineers will be “optimized” rather than eliminated immediately? Reality is both cruel and gentle. Most people will not be fired at once. Their salaries may be compressed. Their work may be outsourced or converted to contract roles. Parts of their responsibilities may be automated. This is gradual optimization, not a purge.

Why, then, might the number of engineers who can use AI actually increase? The conclusion runs against intuition. Engineering jobs will not disappear as AI spreads, but the scale of the systems covered by each engineer will grow exponentially.

One engineer with AI can do what once required a team of five to ten. Small companies become easier to start, more complex systems proliferate, and demand rises for people who can take genuine technical responsibility. The form of the job changes into technical leadership. The job itself does not vanish.

History shows the same pattern in every wave of automation. High-level languages reduced the number of jobs writing assembly while causing the population of software engineers to explode. The web, the cloud, and frameworks reduced operations headcount while producing huge growth in product, backend, and full-stack engineering.

AI-assisted programming is doing the same. The number of engineers who merely execute will decline; demand for systems engineers will rise.

Almost every time, labor at the bottom is compressed while the structure above it expands.

40. Why Are We Talking About Descartes Again in the AI Age?

The person who asked this probably works in AI. Descartes’s central idea can be reduced to one sentence: rebuild the whole system of knowledge from the indubitable subject.

Descartes was not the first person to doubt the world, but he was the first to make systematic doubt a method. He did not doubt a particular conclusion. He doubted everything that could be doubted: the senses, because they might deceive him; experience, because it might be a dream; even mathematics, because an evil demon might make him calculate incorrectly. Doubt was not meant to negate everything. It was a method for finding what could not be doubted.

Descartes’s first certainty is the famous “I think, therefore I am.” After doubting everything, he discovers that even if he doubts the whole world, he cannot doubt the fact that he is doubting.

He therefore reaches the proposition Cogito, ergo sum. The crucial point is not thought alone. It is that the subject becomes the irreducible starting point. My existence no longer depends on confirmation by the world, God, experience, or another person. This is a structural turning point: for the first time, the certainty of existence moves from the world to the subject.

Descartes builds knowledge from the bottom up. I necessarily exist as a thinking subject. I can grasp clear and distinct ideas. God, as a perfect being, will not deceive me systematically. Reason is therefore trustworthy, mathematics is trustworthy, and natural science becomes possible. The world is not trustworthy in advance; it is proved trustworthy through the reason of the subject. The philosophical foundation of modern science lies here.

Why has Descartes become more important in the age of AI? Because many contemporary questions still live in his shadow. Does AI possess an “I think”? Can agency be reduced to computation? Must science turn the world into an object? Is the system replacing the subject? Without understanding Descartes, we cannot fully understand AI, much less ourselves.

41. Why Do Traders Grow Euphoric When Losing and Lost When Winning?

The phenomenon is counterintuitive, but profoundly human.

Many experienced traders admit in private that they felt most exhilarated while standing at the edge of the abyss, and most lost immediately after doing the right thing.

Losing produces intensity because the system has taken complete possession of the person. In a period of loss, a trader often enters a state of extreme concentration. Emotion pulls powerfully, and the world is reduced to winning and losing, rising and falling.

At the neurological level, stress and uncertainty drive frequent releases of dopamine. The trader imagines a clear-eyed struggle while the system is actually leading them by the nose. Loss does not produce reason. It imposes a sense of meaning. Every trade matters. Every judgment seems to prove the self. The world suddenly acquires a direction, an end, and an enemy. The system resolves the trader’s existential anxiety on their behalf.

Profit produces confusion because the system stops supplying meaning. Once profits become consistent, the market no longer responds to how hard the trader feels they are working. Returns are statistical results, not heroic narratives. Good trading is usually boring, repetitive, and restrained. Being right brings no exhilaration. For the first time, the trader faces a question: if I am not fighting, straining, or proving myself, who am I? Confusion follows.

The euphoria of losing is a form of self-instrumentalization. A trader in a losing period turns themselves into a bearer of risk, a decision machine, and fuel for emotion. So long as they can keep trading and struggling, the self remains useful. Paradoxically, being treated as a means makes the person feel useful and valuable. Loss returns the trader to the system as a node. Profit pushes the trader into a more frightening position: a person who must take responsibility for their own existence.

The confusion that follows profit is a vacuum of meaning. Stable returns do not necessarily bring satisfaction. The market stops rewarding emotional impulse and punishes heroic stories instead. The trader discovers that one more unit of money does not make the self more itself. The strategy works, but the trader is not needed. The system operates well, but the individual is replaceable. This is a crisis of success that very few people truly encounter.

Why do many traders eventually destroy themselves? They cannot tolerate the loss of meaning that accompanies profit. They add leverage without reason or change a strategy that was working, deliberately creating volatility and stimulation. They do not misunderstand the risk. They are searching for a familiar feeling: I am still fighting, therefore I still matter.

Loss makes the system need you, and you become euphoric. Profit releases you from the system’s story, and you become confused. The greatest difficulty for a mature trader has never been making money. It is remaining willing to exist without conflict, proof, or excitement.

42. After Vibe Coding, How Can a Product Scale?

This question reaches the heart of the matter. We should begin by admitting a reality: vibe coding has a natural ceiling. It excels at testing quickly whether anyone wants a product, assembling perceptible value at speed, and translating intuition into output with minimal engineering language.

Its structural weakness is an excess of implicit state: global variables, magic parameters, and abstractions that were piled up rather than designed. AI lacks the ability to refuse. It accepts every request and patches every problem until the system collapses.

The problem is not that the code was written badly. The method has completed its mission. Vibe coding is the right answer at the seed-user stage. Continuing to vibe-code during the scaling stage is a structural mistake.

What is the alternative? Think about how a team traditionally operated. Each engineer owned one part. Everyone coordinated, communicated, and held design reviews. In substance, they were modularizing the work.

But modularization does not mean dividing the project among several AI agents and wasting tokens. Ineffective modularization merely divides code into services, utilities, and controllers. Functions grow shorter while files multiply. The code begins to look like a system without acquiring genuine system design.

The real answer is modularizing boundaries.

The first and most important task is to modularize the product boundary. Ask three questions: What does this system explicitly refuse to support? Which requests should receive a firm no? Which user behaviors would we rather lose than accommodate? If those questions cannot be answered, modularization will not reduce complexity. A scalable product is a bounded product. No boundaries mean infinite branches, which means vibe-coding hell.

Second, modularize the boundaries of state. This matters more than modularizing code. Scale is not fundamentally a problem of functions, but of state. Divide the system into explicit state owners. Who owns the source of truth? Who may only read? Who may write? The direction of state changes must be explicit, one-way, traceable, and capable of rejecting invalid changes. A simple test is whether you can draw on a whiteboard which states a user action changes and which states it must never change. If not, you are still only vibe coding.

The most advanced move is to modularize by rate of change. A system that truly scales strongly separates what changes frequently from what changes rarely. UI prompts and forms of expression may change often; core calculations, permissions, and ledgers should change slowly. The goal is not another module. It is physical separation between what may change today and what should remain untouched for six months. Once that division is complete, vibe coding becomes much safer.

A practical path might look like this. First, freeze the code for core functions and mark it clearly: do not refactor, do not optimize, and add no further features here. Second, separate an inviolable core that includes identity, permissions, state, the source of truth, and fundamental business rules. Do not use vibe coding in these areas, or require a human to review 100 percent of any AI-generated change before it is merged. Third, reserve vibe coding for UI and expression layers, disposable functions, and experimental features gated by flags.

Vibe coding is not wrong. It simply should not be mistaken for a systems method. A scalable product is not code that permits more. It is a system in which fewer things are allowed to happen.

Does that mean people are still necessary? Of course. That is precisely why demand for engineers who know how to use AI will be greater than before.

43. Why Does Kant Say That Persons Are Ends and Reason Is Sacred?

This question follows naturally from Descartes, because Kant’s central thought marks the dividing line of modern philosophy.

If you understand why Kant says that persons are ends in themselves, you understand the philosophical basis of modern human rights, the rule of law, and the dignity of the person.

The claim comes from Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals. Kant’s formulation is: act in such a way that you always treat humanity, whether in your own person or in the person of another, as an end and never merely as a means. The crucial word is “merely.”

What is a means? A means can be replaced, compared, consumed, and discarded. Its value depends on its use. A chair is a means. A machine is a means. A person reduced to an instrument is treated as a means.

What is an end? An end has intrinsic value. Its value does not depend on external use, and it cannot simply be exchanged. Kant says that rational beings possess dignity rather than price. A price has an equivalent; dignity does not.

Why do persons have intrinsic value? Because they are capable of legislating for themselves. Kant does not say it is because they possess souls, resemble God, or have emotions. He says that they can give themselves moral law, a capacity he calls autonomy. A being that can understand a rule, reflect on it, and choose to follow it is not merely driven. It engages in moral self-legislation. Such a being cannot properly be treated as a tool.

Why, then, is reason sacred? For Kant, reason is not arithmetic skill, an instrument, or a calculation of efficiency. It is the capacity to set universal law for action without conditions. This is the categorical imperative: act only on a principle that you could will to become universal law. Reason allows a person to step beyond private interest, desire, impulse, and power into universality.

Kant calls reason sacred because, in his era, God was ceasing to be the source of morality. The Church no longer served as unquestioned authority, and tradition no longer provided the foundation. Reason itself became the moral legislator. It inherited the place of God and assumed the role of issuing unconditional commands. Nietzsche would later attack precisely this move.

The AI age brings a new question. If reason is merely computational power, machines will surely become more rational than people. Kantian reason, however, is not calculation. It is the capacity to determine for oneself what ought to be done. The key is not knowing a rule, but acknowledging that the rule binds oneself. It is the capacity for autonomy. At least so far, AI has not demonstrated that kind of self-binding.

Kant says that persons are ends not simply because they have value, but because they legislate value. If value is not a natural fact but must be given through the legislation of an agent, then that agent cannot be instrumentalized.

If algorithms one day establish values, systems optimize rules, and data determine morality, will Kant’s principle that persons are ends still hold? Perhaps this is the greatest philosophical fault line of modernity.

44. Why Is Conflict in France More Violent Yet More Controllable?

If Britain’s problem is that conflict is delayed, culturalized, and moralized until it finally escapes control, France has the opposite problem. Conflict is brought forward, made public, and allowed to become violent. Precisely for that reason, it remains more manageable.

Conflict in France is more violent because the state does not avoid it. It is more controllable because the state draws boundaries in advance. Unlike Britain’s plural society, France has consistently insisted on a republic with a unitary public sphere.

The French premise is that a person has only one identity in public. French political philosophy begins from a nearly nonnegotiable proposition: in the public sphere, you can be only a citizen, not the representative of a culture. This proposition takes institutional form. Religious symbols are not permitted in public schools. The religious neutrality of public office cannot be compromised. The law does not recognize religious law as publicly legitimate. The logic can be summarized in one sentence: the Republic does not negotiate with cultures; it speaks to citizens.

This arrangement makes conflict more violent because France places it in the open from the beginning. The state says no directly. The law prohibits directly. The police intervene directly. Conflict erupts directly in the street. There is none of Britain’s prolonged, ambiguous space for negotiation. Protests are more frequent, clashes more intense, and images from the street less attractive. But pressure is released in discrete episodes rather than accumulated over the long term.

Continuous release makes the pressure more controllable. France does several things that Britain does not dare to do. First, it sets red lines in advance even when doing so attracts condemnation. France tells everyone plainly: what you believe is private, but you cannot carry it into public institutions or demand that those institutions create an exception for you. The conflict is therefore not a contest between identities, but a question of whether someone crossed a boundary. That question can be adjudicated.

Second, conflict is treated as a political question rather than a cultural taboo. In France, one may oppose the politicization of Islam, criticize the public role of religion, or speak even more bluntly without automatically turning the issue into something that cannot be discussed, as often happens in Britain. Extreme positions are exposed earlier, opposition can gather political force, and the state can act in the name of order.

Finally, French state violence is legal, visible, and institutionalized. France does not deny its existence. Riot police, tear gas, water cannons, and arrests are treated as normal tools by which the Republic maintains order. Britain is more inclined to buffer conflict with language, moral appeals, and community representatives. France breaks the illusion first and discusses integration later. Perhaps this helps explain why France had a great revolution while Britain’s transition was much gentler.

What France has actually prevented is not violence but a parallel society. Britain’s effort to avoid direct conflict allowed parallel societies to take shape. France accepted direct conflict in exchange for institutional unity. Its bottom line is: we can argue every day, but the state has only one set of rules. That makes it difficult for community autonomy, religious law, or cultural exceptions to acquire institutional depth.

France is often misunderstood as less tolerant. The reverse may be true. It refuses to turn tolerance into an institutional concession. It permits people to differ in private, but refuses to make those differences variables in the public order.

France accepts intense conflict in the short term in exchange for institutional clarity in the long term. The path may be undignified, ungentle, and unpopular with elite opinion. But it has one absolute advantage: it does not outsource value judgment to time.

45. Why Can Entrepreneurship Not Avoid Philosophy?

The person who asked this may have thought I was neglecting my proper work. Many people assume that entrepreneurship is about cash flow, products, growth, and fundraising. What could any of that have to do with philosophy? My view is that the longer one remains an entrepreneur, the less one can avoid it.

After nearly twenty years of learning from and observing Silicon Valley, I believe the essence of entrepreneurship is not making a product. It is answering a question about value.

The first-principles question is not how to build a feature, acquire users, or raise money. It is why this thing deserves to exist. That is already philosophy. What is value? What is fairness? What is efficiency? What is freedom?

Uber changed the philosophy of ownership versus use. Facebook changed the question of whether connection amounts to understanding. Afterpay changed the question of whether consumption should be brought forward in time.

At Jarsy, the company I am building, the central question is who should have access to pre-IPO opportunities. Is access to finance a matter of identity or a technical problem? The first issue is not commercial. It is a question in the philosophy of value.

Entrepreneurship is an act of constructing a worldview. A startup does not merely make a product. It builds a worldview that can run. A founder’s underlying philosophy determines whether they see users as tools or as persons. It determines whether capital is a means or an end, whether regulation is an enemy or a partner in coexistence, and whether growth should prioritize scale or stability.

Elon Musk’s philosophy is technological determinism. Mark Zuckerberg’s is the primacy of connection. Steve Jobs’s is that aesthetics is truth. Philosophy determines direction, and direction determines results.

Entrepreneurship without philosophy is eventually swallowed by the market. If a founder cannot answer “What is the ultimate meaning of what I am doing?” “Would I continue if money were no object?” or “Would I retreat if regulatory resistance became enormous?” then the founder can only follow the market’s short-term signals.

The market has no philosophy. It has only price. Price pulls founders toward chasing trends, telling stories, competing for financing, and sacrificing long-term trust. The company may eventually make money while the founder becomes lost. We have already asked why many traders grow more confused as they earn more. They lack a philosophy of ends.

I see entrepreneurship as an existential practice. It answers an ontological question: “Who will I become as an individual in this era?” A founder may become an opportunist, a changer of structures, a setter of rules, a repairer of systems, or something else. Each position corresponds to a different philosophy.

Marcuse asked how systems flatten individuals. Kant said that persons are ends, not merely means. Nietzsche said, “Become who you are.” Without philosophy, entrepreneurship can easily treat people as engagement and trust as a conversion rate.

The deeper reason is that entrepreneurship forces a founder to confront the void. When markets deteriorate, fundraising fails, user growth stalls, the media criticizes, and regulation tightens, the founder suddenly asks: why am I doing this at all? Technology cannot provide the answer. Public relations cannot provide it. Even investors cannot provide it. Only the founder’s own understanding of the world can.

I work on tokenization, pre-IPO accessibility, and compliance-first infrastructure. Behind those keywords is a philosophy of value: access to finance should be more equal; technology can lower barriers effectively; rules are not enemies, but sources of order.

Entrepreneurs do not discuss philosophy in order to look profound. They do so because entrepreneurship means running one’s worldview in reality.

Steve Jobs once described businesspeople who cared only about quarterly earnings as people who “sold sugar water.” Those who truly want to change the world, he believed, must work at the intersection of the humanities and technology. As he famously said upon returning to Apple in 1997: “The people who are crazy enough to think they can change the world are the ones who do.”

46. What Is the Frankfurt School?

I mention the Frankfurt School often because I genuinely admire it. The school was an intellectual community of social scientists, philosophers, and cultural critics centered on the Institute for Social Research at the University of Frankfurt in Germany.

It was one of the twentieth century’s most important traditions of critical theory. Its thinkers returned continually to one central question: in modern society, how can a person avoid being swallowed by the system?

The central figures of the first generation included Max Horkheimer, Theodor W. Adorno, and Walter Benjamin. They asked why the proletarian revolution predicted by Marx had not occurred, and why reason and Enlightenment had produced fascism and totalitarianism instead.

They shifted Marxism from economic determinism toward a critique of culture and ideology, and developed the concept of instrumental reason. Yes, it is the same “instrumental” we have been discussing. Dialectic of Enlightenment argues that Enlightenment reason contains a logic of self-destruction within itself. Their judgment of modern society was therefore profoundly pessimistic.

The second generation shifted from negation toward communication and became somewhat more hopeful. Its central figure was Jürgen Habermas. The first generation believed that reason itself had degenerated into instrumental reason. Habermas argued that reason had not degenerated, but that systems had reduced it to a single instrumental form.

He therefore developed the concept of communicative rationality, distinguished the lifeworld from the system, and elaborated a theory of the public sphere. His major works include The Structural Transformation of the Public Sphere and The Theory of Communicative Action. The spirit of the second generation was to defend modernity rather than reject it completely. If the first generation centered on pessimistic critique, the second centered on institutional reconstruction.

The third generation focuses on culture, agency, and recognition. Its central figures include Axel Honneth, Nancy Fraser, and Rainer Forst. Its principal subjects are theories of recognition and justice, identity politics, and democratic legitimacy. Honneth argues that modern conflict concerns relations of recognition rather than relations of production. Fraser argues that justice is a matter of both redistribution and recognition. By this stage, the Frankfurt School had moved beyond philosophy narrowly understood and deep into political philosophy.

Many major contemporary thinkers remain active, including Honneth, Fraser, and Forst. Hartmut Rosa’s theory of social acceleration, familiar to many readers through the concept of technological acceleration, updates the first generation’s cultural pessimism.

Byung-Chul Han, whom we have discussed before, does not belong to the Frankfurt School, but clearly inherits the spirit of its first-generation cultural critique. He shifts attention from instrumental reason to self-optimization, the achievement society, and digital exposure.

If we had to summarize the Frankfurt School in one sentence,

it would be that across a century,

its thinkers have continued to ask

how people in modern society can preserve agency

without being swallowed by the system.

That is why I value the Frankfurt School so highly.

47. Why Is Captain America So Popular?

If Tony Stark is the first Avenger to reject the logic of efficiency, Steve Rogers is the first to reject the presumption of institutional legitimacy.

Steve is not useful at the beginning. He is selected for the super-soldier serum not because he is strong, but because he is weak and still refuses to submit. His most important line is not “I want to enlist.” It is “I don’t like bullies.”

The structure of that sentence matters. He does not say, “I love my country,” “I want to win the war,” or “I want to become a hero.” He says that he cannot accept a particular structure of relationship. This protects the most fundamental level and already anticipates Self-as-an-End.

Steve is also the one person willing to reject a lawful command. The real break occurs in Captain America: The Winter Soldier. S.H.I.E.L.D. plans to use algorithms to predict future threats and eliminate them in advance. From the system’s perspective, the plan is safer, more efficient, more stable, and more likely to benefit the greatest number. But Steve says, “This isn’t freedom. This is fear.”
